I maintained that my economy had succeeded, since fewer vessels were broken. Rosa informed me that this was a novel definition of economy. She brought sweet tea without charging for it. I never discovered whether that omission was kindness or an error.

### E03-017 | The Summer with No Answer | 1875-07

I wrote Henry a new letter about the limits of bodily scale. It took three drafts to remove a paragraph asking whether he had forgotten me. Kappel carried it into town because I had begun to dislike being seen at the post office. No answer came that summer. I interpreted the silence as contempt, then as busyness, then as deliberate caution. None of these interpretations could be tested. I filed a fourth draft, unsent, which contained only a question about his present work and a description of the Mermaid's habits. When autumn arrived I burned the draft but kept the envelope. I was proud of not pursuing him. Pride is a convenient name for a decision one cannot entirely defend.

### E17-002 | The Gentleman Who Was Not My Type | 1875-07

Reiss introduced me to a retired merchant at an outdoor table. The gentleman's chief intellectual accomplishment was the possession of a watch which chimed a quarter-hour inaccurately. He was also plainly interested in my company, an interest communicated through attentions too numerous to mistake. I attempted an elaborate description of my microscopic work to discourage him. Reiss watched the performance with visible amusement. On the way back he said I could simply have declined an invitation. I answered that plain refusal was frequently mistaken for an insult. Reiss said that my alternative had been an insult with diagrams. I considered whether the man's attention had displeased me because it was unwanted or because I disliked being seen to receive it. These were different questions. I did not settle either. At our next meeting I spoke to the merchant courteously, and he discovered my research tedious. We parted on terms that would have been difficult to improve through further analysis.

### E06-010 | The Last Line on the Page | 1875-09

Kappel had begun preserving my unsent letters to Henry between folders of neglected dissertations. I found the arrangement impertinent. A letter not sent, I observed, was not correspondence. It was draft material, and draft material belonged to its author. Kappel answered that my concern for ownership had never prevented me from requesting access to other men's discarded thoughts. Mathilde was present to collect the paper inventory. She counted four letters bearing Henry's name, each one shorter than its predecessor until the last consisted of a single unfinished question. I took all four home. Two weeks later I returned the first three to Kappel's archive, keeping the last in the blue ledger. I had no wish to place its question in another man's custody, even one who had long since earned the privilege of seeing worse things in me.

### E06-011 | The Little Examination | 1876-02

Mathilde brought six blank cards, each bearing a different shape, and requested permission to hold them near the Queen's chamber in an order unknown to me. I consented because a result that arrives only when its author directs the sequence is a party trick, not knowledge. The Queen approached a dark triangle twice, avoided it once, and ignored the rest. The pattern did not survive the afternoon. Mathilde was disappointed. I was furious, but chiefly with the cards, which had been cut from inferior stock and bent in the damp. Lenz had prepared a second batch. We repeated the sequence on another day with no improvement. I recorded the failure and refused Mathilde's suggestion that we name the Queen's favorite shape. If I must bestow titles upon insignificant things, there are members of the faculty still available.

### E11-014 | The Hand That Would Not Settle | 1876-03

Lenz's hands trembled slightly after a long day at the furnace, and I noticed it while he attempted a delicate repair. I asked whether he had been drinking. He showed me the blister beneath his thumb and suggested that I try holding the hot rod myself before diagnosing his habits. Clara bound the hand. We postponed the work. At the next visit I brought a padded grip devised from cork and cloth. It was a poor contrivance but useful enough that he kept it. He thanked me with a joke about my becoming an apprentice at last. I replied that necessity had forced the descent. I did not inquire whether the grip lessened his pain; I watched his fingers while he worked and could see that it did. In my notebooks I recorded the alteration to the instrument. I did not record the reason I had designed it.

### E22-013 | A Disagreement over the Keys | 1876-03

I returned from an errand to find Clara in the upper room and the floor dry where a broken tap might have ruined a month's work. She had entered with Lenz's key, which I considered a trespass until she showed me the warped stopcock. I asked why she had not waited. She asked which of the drowned creatures she should have consulted for permission. Her coat smelled of wet wool and lamp soot. We spent the next hour drafting conditions under which she might enter in my absence, and I insisted on two signatures and an exact account of the keys. She signed without reading my most magnificent clauses. A second key hung afterward in her dispensary. I complained for weeks about the breach of scientific sovereignty. I also slept more easily on nights when the pipes knocked.

### E03-018 | The Repairman's Hand | 1876-04

Lenz injured two fingers on hot glass and for a fortnight could not make the joints upon which my experiments depended. I arrived at his shop armed with instructions about splints. His wife put a stack of unwrapped orders in front of me and said I might assist by keeping my medical imagination to myself. I wrapped glass, mislaid a label, and discovered that paper twine can humiliate a philosopher as thoroughly as a hostile faculty. I returned the next week and did the work with fewer mistakes. When Lenz offered to reduce my bill, I refused. I am not fond of debts whose amount cannot be entered in a ledger; moreover, I had no wish to teach him that injury reduced the value of his skill. His wife later sent better twine to my tower, with no explanation.

### E14-008 | The Address on the Funeral Notice | 1876-04

Mathilde Rosen brought a proof of a funeral notice to Georg because a printer's compositor had omitted the street number. I corrected the punctuation before she had finished explaining why the omission mattered. Mathilde told me that the family had already lost one relative who had been unable to find the new house; a misplaced mourner was not a metaphor but a person. Georg took the notice and checked it against the parish records. I asked Mathilde why she tolerated work so dull. She said that the dead rarely complained and the living complained quite enough for both parties. We walked back to the printing shop through puddles containing fragments of election posters. I bought a packet of envelopes I did not require, then asked her to send future proofs of my scientific pamphlets directly to the tower. She asked whether that meant I trusted her corrections. I said it meant I distrusted everybody else's. She regarded this as an adequate contractual answer, if not an especially gracious one.

### E06-012 | The Name in the Catalogue | 1876-06

A catalogue listed my private pamphlet under 'Pretorian curiosities,' a designation at once illiterate and insulting. The shop's apprentice had entered the heading from a secondhand dealer's note. Mathilde offered to correct it. I told her not to trouble herself, since a collector stupid enough to purchase curiosities would at least pay an honest price for the book. That evening I wrote the dealer an impeccably polite letter containing four separate demonstrations of his incompetence. Kappel later saw it and observed that I had made myself memorable to a man who might otherwise have forgotten me. He was quite right. Weeks afterward the corrected catalogue arrived with the category 'experimental natural philosophy.' I placed it between the uncorrected proof and my letter, a triptych considerably more satisfying than a public apology.

### E06-013 | Ink after the Burn | 1876-09

Lenz's injured hand recovered well enough for work, though he could no longer close the little finger without discomfort. Mathilde brought him a ledger ruling device as a gift, only to discover that he preferred drawing lines with a straightedge held in the other hand. He showed her the small changes he had made to his bench. I remarked that adaptation of an instrument was preferable to romantic mourning over its limitations. Lenz asked whether I regarded the human hand as an instrument. I said I regarded it as an expensive apparatus insufficiently appreciated by its owner. He laughed, then used his uninjured hand to produce a new chamber stop precisely fitted to my request. I retained the earlier flawed stop as well. It reminded me that a workshop can change more reliably than a university, although I did not flatter either craftsman with the comparison.

### E03-019 | The Ledger's Missing Page | 1876-11

I found one page missing from the blue ledger, the page on which I had entered the Queen's first sustained orientation. My immediate suspicion fell upon a visiting instrument seller. Clara pointed out that the binding had worn thin from years of use. Kappel, summoned like a magistrate to a crime scene, found the leaf pressed inside a botanical catalogue where I had used it as a marker. The relief was brief; the embarrassment, more durable. We began copying observations into a second register kept in town. I resented the duplication until a water leak ruined several loose papers that winter. The copy preserved the records. The leaf itself, still bearing the crease of its escape, remained in the first book.

### E20-008 | The Blind Naming | 1876-11

I claimed I could distinguish the promising vessels at a glance. Clara said this was the kind of claim made by men who never allowed anyone to rearrange a shelf. Lenz tied blue ribbons to the lids, marked the bottoms, and asked me to select the soundest specimens while the shutters stood half closed. I chose the most attractive three. Two had already failed. The plain vessel which I passed over continued its obscure growth for another month. I accused Lenz of allowing the light to mislead me. She opened the shutters and invited me to repeat the trial. I did better, though not well enough to justify the original boast. Lenz wrote the scores and showed no particular delight in defeating me. I found her lack of triumph peculiarly difficult to resent.

### E14-009 | The Queen at the Locked Door | 1876-12

The Queen had begun approaching the narrow service door whenever voices sounded beyond it. I proposed that she sought a particular frequency in Clara's speech. Matthias, present by invitation I had almost regretted issuing, wondered whether she simply expected company. I said the distinction was not testable in its present form. Clara offered to conduct a silent entrance. The Queen approached the door before Clara was visible and withdrew when the hinge stuck. We repeated the trial, and the results were neither beautiful nor conclusive. Matthias asked whether I had ever opened the door merely to see where she wished to go. I reminded him that the room beyond contained instruments, chemical baths, and hazards she could not understand. He conceded the danger, then asked who had decided that confinement was the only safe arrangement. I disliked the implication and wrote down the question. The next morning I placed a small empty passage between two enclosed platforms. The Queen inspected it for ten minutes, then returned to her original place. A refusal to travel is not an endorsement of the prison.

### E06-014 | The Complaint behind Glass | 1877-01

One winter morning the King struck the glass with unusual regularity, stopping whenever the Archbishop approached. I suspected an exchange of signals and said as much. Clara thought the chamber too dry. Mathilde asked whether either hypothesis could explain why the behavior began only after the new stopper had been fitted. I examined the joint and discovered a narrow draft where the glass met the wax. There was nothing heroic in repairing it. The King ceased his tapping that evening; the Archbishop returned to his customary occupation of disapproval. Clara asked whether I would amend the record. I entered her suggested cause beside mine, with the date and no preferred conclusion. I then spent a quarter hour watching the King sit perfectly still, an ingrate with no concern whatever for the labor he had occasioned.

### E14-010 | A Sermon Without My Name | 1877-03

Matthias invited me to a lecture on charity delivered by a visiting prelate. I attended because I was assured no collection would be taken, which proved untrue. The speaker denounced people who pursued knowledge without regard for suffering. He had never heard of me, yet several listeners turned their heads in my direction, which suggested the local imagination required little prompting. I rose to ask how much suffering the institution had prevented by refusing inquiry into its causes. The prelate replied with an account of his hospital work, naming patients and expenses in embarrassing detail. I was prepared for piety, not figures. Afterward Georg said I had asked a fair question and received a fair answer. Matthias said an opponent need not be imaginary merely because one preferred him that way. I contributed money to the hospital fund and gave my name as a misspelling of Pretorius so no one would infer a conversion. The receipt arrived under the wrong spelling and I kept it. I cannot say why.

### E17-003 | The Letter with the Familiar Hand | 1877-03

A letter from Theodor arrived after years in which our correspondence had dwindled to notes at Christmas. He had secured a position supervising architectural drawings and was living with a man whose patience, he said, exceeded that of most professors. The sentence was not an accusation, though I spent a whole afternoon searching it for one. He enclosed a photograph of a building whose windows he had designed. It was handsome and insufficiently ventilated. I prepared a reply listing the defect, then put the sheet away. Clara, bringing fresh cloth for the jars, found me looking at the picture. I told her that the work was competently done. She asked whether I had said as much to him. I wrote him three sentences of praise and an additional paragraph about the windows. He answered in kind, correcting a detail I had misunderstood about the plan. We had lost the ease of our earlier life. We had not lost the pleasure of being exact together.

### E06-015 | A Newspaper Death | 1877-05

An obituary reported the death of a minor scholar who had once signed the petition for my removal. I recognized the signature with precision I could not summon for the man's face. Mathilde asked whether I should send his widow a letter. I answered that condolences from the victim of her husband's cowardice might be mistaken for triumph. Kappel said that perhaps I was afraid of precisely the opposite. We let the paper lie on the table while the press rattled. Later, passing the house where the dead man had lodged as a student, I discovered that I remembered him lending me an umbrella during a storm. I wrote no letter. I did, however, retrieve the damp obituary from the shop's rubbish and file it beside the petition. A death had not altered the signature; it had made the attached hand less conveniently abstract.

### E06-016 | A Volume Too Small to Burn | 1877-07

Mathilde bound the private record into a narrow volume in brown cloth. It contained no formulae, only dates, corrections, the names of those who had looked, and the things my small people had done when watched and when left alone. She called it a book of witnesses. I had intended a vindication. The distinction displeased me, though the paper was excellent. Clara ordered a copy for her own shelf, promising neither praise nor concealment. Kappel asked that a third be kept beyond Ingolstadt. I refused until Mathilde pointed out that I had already lost a page and nearly lost several years of correspondence. We placed a sealed copy with a dealer on the road west. At the end of the afternoon the mechanical bird, still unreliably musical, whistled once from the high shelf. It seemed an absurd fanfare, which did not prevent me from enjoying it.

### E03-020 | The Door Left Open | 1877-08

Lenz repaired the upper landing and, for the first time in years, we could carry a chair to the east window without fearing the floor. Clara opened the shutters. The road below was full of dust and the smell of cut grass, not the host of persecutors I had sometimes imagined. She asked whether I intended to spend the remainder of my life watching the world from a tower. I told her that I had plenty of work. She did not disagree. That evening I prepared a small travelling case with space for one vessel, a notebook and the metronome. I did not yet choose a destination. The case stood unopened beneath the window for months, an intention too delicate to be dignified with the name of a plan.

### E22-014 | The Unpublished Defeat | 1877-08

Mathilde appeared with a copied paragraph in her careful hand. I had written to a correspondent that a specimen's movement survived without a certain light source. My own ledger suggested that it had survived only when I failed to measure the light properly. I wished to annotate the ledger and consider the problem at leisure. Mathilde had already addressed an envelope. She informed me that the recipient possessed an incorrect claim with my name beneath it. I sent the correction, but refused her proposal for a printed notice that would have satisfied the most vindictive conscience. We argued over the sealing wax until the glass inkstand tipped and stained the tablecloth. The stain remains a livid blue. I recall it whenever someone praises the unqualified honesty of scientific men, an opinion ordinarily advanced by men who have not published results.

### E14-011 | The Paper Skeleton | 1877-10

Leonie brought Georg a paper skeleton intended for an educational exhibition. The figure had brass fasteners at the joints and an absurdly cheerful grin painted upon the skull. A teacher had refused it because children found the movable jaw entertaining. I asked why entertainment made the anatomy less accurate. Leonie said the teacher feared that the pupils would remember laughter instead of instruction. I moved the arms and demonstrated that the shoulder had been cut incorrectly. Georg suggested we repair it before insulting the teacher. We spent the afternoon adjusting joints with needles and bits of card. When the model finally raised both arms without tearing, Leonie bowed to it as though acknowledging a new stage performer. I laughed, then arranged the skull upon a chair to inspect the shadow cast by the lamp. For a moment it appeared to be considering an answer to a question no one had asked. I purchased the figure from Leonie. Later visitors assumed it was a morbid ornament. I did not trouble to correct them unless they touched the joints.

### E11-015 | The Invitation I Kept | 1877-11

After the late summer when I had finally allowed the tower door to remain open to visitors, invitations became a nuisance of surprising frequency. Lenz asked me to a family supper; Clara enclosed a note that no specimens would be discussed at the table. I wrote a refusal based on a perfectly real series of observations. Before sending it I found an old invitation from five years earlier among the bills, its corners worn by repeated handling. The experiment was not time-sensitive. I attended the supper with a bottle of gin I considered a respectable offering and discovered that Lenz's daughter had become quite opinionated about my manners. She asked why I always arrived wearing a coat suitable for an execution. I told her it improved the appearance of a happy gathering by contrast. She laughed. On returning to the tower I filed the invitation with correspondence rather than accounts. I laid the invitation among Kappel's letters, though it contained no argument or observation at all.

### E04-001 | The Case Opened at Last | 1878-03

The travelling case had sat beneath the east window for months, becoming such a fixture that Clara threatened to fill it with winter linen. I took the train to Munich and examined a collection of preserved plants, having entrusted the lamps to Lenz and the creatures' arrangements to Clara. I removed the old cracked metronome at the station when its weight offended me; a cherished error need not travel first class. Most of the collection was disappointing. One conservator, however, showed me how to record changes in illumination without disturbing a specimen. I copied his method and improved his chart on the journey home. The Queen had survived my absence without apparent distress. I called this proof that my arrangements were excellent. It was also a somewhat chilly welcome.

### E20-009 | The Garden of Discarded Shapes | 1878-05

Clara entered the neglected glass room and found three cultures I had labelled worthless. Their intended shapes had come to nothing. Yet the moss beside one vessel had changed its color, and another had preserved moisture in a place that ordinarily dried by noon. We could not tell which effect belonged to the growth itself and which to my careless storage. I instructed her to discard the whole collection. She asked whether I proposed destroying the observations as well. I kept five vessels and called the result an ecological trial, a term sufficiently grand to conceal that I had been wrong about rubbish. They occupied the west shelf for years. No little man emerged from them, and it would be false to claim they were our finest specimens. They taught me more about the room than any mirror-polished head ever had.

### E22-015 | The Case of Small Necessities | 1878-07

Marta Voss arrived with a botanical press, worn gloves, and questions so unromantic that I almost sent her away. She wished to know how often the water was changed, what the room smelled like before dawn, and whether the occupants behaved differently after a week of quiet. I had prepared an explanation of their extraordinary origins. She interrupted it to ask whether a damaged latch frightened them. We inspected the lower chambers first. Dried thyme from her satchel scented the room, masking the familiar sourness of the jars, and I had to admit that I had ceased noticing the latter. I gave her the maintenance book, not the secret notebook. The distinction seemed elegant. She returned it covered with marginal inquiries, every one about the stubborn business of keeping something alive. I answered them all, though not in the order she preferred.

### E04-002 | The Botanist with Ink on Her Sleeve | 1878-09

Marta Voss had catalogued a botanical collection dismissed as too difficult for a woman by three gentlemen who could not identify half its specimens. I met her over a disputed entry. She showed me a dormant preparation recovered after a longer interval than I would have predicted, then produced the measurements before I could accuse her of relying on anecdote. Better still, she had read an unsigned paper of mine and found a fault in its conclusion without insulting its observations. At luncheon I asked for her address. She required, as a condition of correspondence, that I cease using the word 'provincial' to mean 'contrary to Pretorius.' I accepted no such constitutional amendment, but posted her a packet within the fortnight. She answered it, and in ink dark enough to be a challenge.

### E14-012 | A Small Funeral for a Name | 1878-11

A man named Bernhard Vogel was buried on a morning so windy the priest's pages would not remain in order. I had never met him. He had copied some of my observations for a private catalogue and once sent a correction with the modest request that his name not be printed. Marta Voss remarked that he might have wished to protect his employment. Georg knew the family and said Vogel had disliked crowds in life. I went because his correction had saved me from an error in a botanical comparison, and because I thought attendance might be the only acknowledgement left available. There were eleven mourners, counting me. No one recognized my connection to the deceased, which initially offended me and then seemed appropriate. After the service I placed the corrected page inside my coat rather than leaving it on the grave. Voss asked what I had said to his widow. Nothing, I replied. She had enough people telling her what sort of man her husband had been. I thought it sufficient to have come. I could not persuade Voss that I meant this modestly.

### E22-016 | The Inventory Left Unfinished | 1878-12

Rosa spread flour sacks and invoices across the table while Voss examined the newest catalogue. Between them they had turned my pantry into an audit. I could account for every pane of glass, and nearly every spoonful of spirit used in preserving instruments. Voss asked where I had recorded the Queen's preference for the shabby paper corner. I said that preferences were not provisions. Rosa asked why I had purchased three yards of velvet if they were not. I began a separate page headed 'Unquantified Requirements' and hated it immediately. The ink blotted. I left the next line empty rather than pretend I could finish the category before supper. Years later, I found myself adding notes to the same page under different names. I have never enjoyed an inventory that records my ignorance so accurately.

## A tower connected to the world

### E04-003 | The Letter in Three Hands | 1879-01

Voss returned my paper annotated in three hands, two inks and a pencil so sharp it had torn the margin. I objected that an argument ought at least to show its owner. Clara suggested that three naturalists might simply have worked at one table, a thought disappointingly plausible. The annotations challenged my interpretation of a creature moving toward a favorable condition as proof of preference. I wrote eleven pages in defense, discovered that the ninth contradicted the third, and sent two pages with additional observations. Voss wrote 'Useful' beneath the amended table. Heller had once bestowed the same parsimonious blessing. I had not expected a young botanist to revive the old irritation quite so efficiently. I asked her for the next objection before supper.

### E14-013 | The Experiment That Would Not Flatter Me | 1879-02

Voss arrived with botanical drawings which showed a relation between growth and enclosure less straightforward than I had predicted. Clara prepared three controlled vessels, each supplied with the same medium but with a different arrangement of light. I favored the narrowest one on aesthetic grounds: the subject stood at its center like a figure upon a stage. It also produced the poorest growth. Voss repeated the work, obtained the same disappointment, and wrote the measurements without comment. I claimed the arrangement had been damaged in transit. Clara pointed out that the alleged damage was present in all three vessels. After supper I revised the drawing, leaving a narrower stage only for observations that required isolation. Voss never asked me to confess error. She sent a cleaner drawing by post and marked the relevant dimensions in green ink. It was a superior form of victory. Had she demanded an apology, I should perhaps have defended the foolish apparatus another year.

### E23-001 | The Scent That Returned | 1879-03

Voss placed a sprig of thyme beside a cracked drain. A small inhabitant who had been motionless crossed the chamber in a sudden hurry. I called it an interest in botany. Voss reminded me that the same smell had been present when a vessel leaked the previous summer. I searched the old entries and found an account of frightened movement recorded as an effect of temperature. The room still smelled of wet plaster; verdigris showed beneath the copper basin. I had been so pleased with the neatness of my old explanation that I had failed to notice the neighboring detail. We restored the original arrangement without moving the cutting, then removed the cutting alone. The response changed. My notebook acquired the title 'Associations Not of My Choosing.' Voss borrowed it for a fortnight and returned it with more pages marked than I considered courteous.

### E04-004 | The Picnic No One Had Planned | 1879-07

Voss came through Ingolstadt on business, and Clara arranged for all four of us to eat outdoors as if such a thing required no justification. Lenz brought eggs wrapped in newspaper. Voss carried a specimen box and placed it beneath the shade, then proceeded to speak for an hour about village gardens rather than botany. I disliked how swiftly she and Clara agreed that my appetite could not be trusted. Lenz spent most of the afternoon watching his daughter run at the edge of the water. For a moment I envied him the ease with which he could be both craftsman and father. Then the child asked me how a clock knew it was morning, and I was permitted to answer a question more pleasant than anything in my correspondence. Her father said I had frightened her only a little.

### E17-004 | A Bed for a Visitor | 1879-09

Reiss came to the tower with a damaged valise and asked whether he might stay two nights while a difficult question was settled at his school. I gave him the west room, chiefly because it was the only chamber in which the roof was not leaking. He told me an acquaintance had threatened to expose the correspondence between himself and another man. I asked whether the letters were authentic. He said they were. I advised him not to purchase silence from someone who could demand further payment. He pointed out that I possessed a locked gate, independent income sufficient for my habits, and almost no institutional reputation left to lose. He did not. I sent Mathilde a note asking her to retain his papers in a sealed envelope, should he choose to deposit them. Reiss went away without using the offer. For weeks afterward I checked the west-room window latch before bed, although the room was empty. I did not regard myself as responsible for his security. The latch received considerable attention nevertheless.

### E23-002 | The Keeper of the False Relic | 1879-10

A gentleman offered me a splinter of bone in a silver mount, assuring me that it had moved beneath a medium's hand. I took it to Georg because he knew old cases and their ordinary dishonest repairs. He turned the mount over and recognized the velvet beneath it as a strip his father had sold three summers earlier. The bone may have been venerable; the stage upon which it performed was not. I purchased the affair for less than its owner expected and placed it in a cabinet beside a broken clock and a portrait whose eyes appeared to follow visitors. The gentlemen who inspected these objects invariably chose the relic as the most impressive. I allowed the deception to proceed for a minute before showing the stitching. This was not kindness. Their faces became much more interesting after the revelation.

### E04-005 | The Mirror Kept Covered | 1880-02

The Devil had for years bowed to a looking glass with such magnificent affectation that I could not bear to deprive him of it. Voss uncovered the glass at irregular intervals and found that he sometimes approached, sometimes attended instead to the King's disturbances. She proposed that he liked movement rather than his own reflection. I objected that the two are scarcely enemies: vanity is often an appetite for a moving likeness. Our first arrangements could not separate the possibilities. We devised a less flattering test with changes of light and angle, and the creature continued to frustrate us. I retained his name. When he bowed again I bowed back, to Voss's evident despair. One need not resolve the mechanism of a performance to appreciate its style.

### E20-010 | The Uniform Face | 1880-02

Lenz brought a finely graduated gauge and asked why I had copied the same facial proportions into six designs. I said that an intelligent artist does not abandon a solution because it has proved successful. He spread the results on the table: six refined faces, none possessed of the ordinary signs of continued life. The proportions were not the only variable, as he knew, but they were the part I had refused to reconsider. We worked a week altering the drawings. The revised shape offended me and performed no better. I ought to have been pleased that the evidence spared my aesthetic principle. Instead I found myself irritated that the evidence remained insufficient to vindicate anything. Lenz returned the gauge with his name marked discreetly inside the case. I made a note of the inscription and never mistook the instrument for my invention.

### E23-003 | An Ugly Room for a Lovely Specimen | 1880-04

The north room had limewash falling from one corner in curls like dead petals. I intended to remake it in white tile and bright metal, having recently found a specimen of exquisite form whose habits were irregular. Clara moved the chamber away from its polished mirror while the men repaired the shutters. For two weeks the creature slept consistently. When we restored the mirror, it woke and remained awake. A man more devoted to simple conclusions might have inferred an aversion to reflections. I was devoted to finding an explanation that would preserve the mirror, which had cost a great deal. Eventually I covered it with a tin sheet. Clara kept the renovation estimate folded in the account book. I could tell she enjoyed the fact that an ugly room had improved the experiment. I gave her no such satisfaction openly.

### E14-014 | The Man Who Asked to Be Afraid | 1880-05

Matthias sent word that a parishioner, recently widowed, wished to speak with me about the possibility of restoring a dead husband. I refused twice. On the third request I visited, thinking I might at least correct the village rumors. The woman asked whether what I had made in jars could remember a person's love. I said that I had made small lives, not preserved missing husbands. She asked whether I was certain. For once I answered that certainty was unnecessary where no evidence supported the hope. Matthias sat by the window and did not interfere. After the woman left he said I had spared her. I objected that I had merely described the limits of my results. He accepted my objection, which deprived me of an argument I had prepared. Georg later told me that she had gone home disappointed but no longer intending to sell her house to finance a charlatan. This should have pleased me more than it did. There are limits which sound remarkably like defeat even when they are accurate.

### E04-006 | A Copy Sent Without Permission | 1880-10

Kappel sent Voss a copy of an essay I had marked private. Her reply demolished a paragraph I had once considered unassailable. For several hours I was enchanted by the criticism, then remembered the insult to my ownership. I descended upon Kappel with a fury rehearsed all the way to town. He produced my original instruction: preserve the paper. I had forbidden its destruction, not its circulation. An omission, he said, was not a lock. I disliked him most when he was exact. I wrote Voss that the copy had travelled without my express permission and that her criticisms were nonetheless welcome. This was very nearly an apology, if read by a forgiving eye. She answered with further corrections. I filed them together, so that no one could mistake who had provoked the correspondence.

### E23-004 | The Tale Told at a Proper Table | 1880-11

Mathilde served boiled fish and a plum preserve so sharp that it punished the tongue before one could admire it. Voss asked what had become of the handsome specimen I had displayed in a drawing. I offered an account of its promising proportions. She asked a second time. The fish cooled. I admitted it had not survived a modest alteration I had made in pursuit of further symmetry. No one expressed the horror I had prepared to rebuke. Mathilde asked how many attempts I had made afterward. The number was disagreeable. I gave it anyway, and conversation improved. Thereafter those two ladies developed the habit of asking for 'the other version' of any report I presented too elegantly. Their impertinence has preserved several useful facts, while rendering dinner a less restful institution than I had imagined.

### E14-015 | The Seat Reserved for Nobody | 1881-04

I visited Leonie on the anniversary of no particular event and found a second cup waiting beside her sewing machine. She said she had expected me because it was Thursday and because I had visited on the two previous Thursdays. I objected to being converted into a custom. She said customs were simply repeated decisions stripped of their vanity. The old hall had installed brighter lamps, and she no longer worked there regularly. I offered to hire her to construct a miniature backdrop for the King, whose taste for arrangements of colored paper persisted. She declined, not because the task was beneath her but because her hands had begun to stiffen before evening. We drank tea. Georg's funeral register lay open on her table, awaiting new binding. She was doing a favor for him and would not charge. I told her it was poor economics. She asked whether I paid her for the tea. I said that was scarcely the same thing. The second cup stayed in its accustomed place while we argued.

### E04-007 | The Prague Clockmaker | 1881-05

I travelled to Prague to meet Otto Faber, whose pendulums were said to resist changes in humidity better than those of his rivals. He listened politely to my complaints about the cracked metronome, then asked why I continued to use an instrument I did not trust. I said I had learned from its failure. He answered that I might now learn from a better one. His workshop smelled of beeswax and brass filings. I bought a calibrated timing device and kept the old metronome as a memento rather than forcing it to perform work for which it was no longer fit. When Lenz examined the new mechanism, he praised Faber's joints before mentioning its price. I was irritated to discover that a purchase could be both extravagant and correct.

### E23-005 | A Sunday without Results | 1881-05

Rosa stopped me on the river path with a basket of pears and a package too heavy for a woman who refused to admit that her knee troubled her. I said I was bound home to record a specimen's afternoon movements. She asked whether the specimen had agreed to an appointment. I carried the package to her sister's door. We ate a pear apiece on the return journey, the skins bitter beneath the sweetness, while bees worked the bank and my shoes accumulated mud. We spoke of a neighbor's roof, an unpromising horse, and the increasing cost of lamp oil. I returned after sunset. Nothing had happened in the chamber that could not be observed the following day. I resisted the temptation to call the excursion restorative. It was merely time spent living in a world that declined to announce results at hourly intervals.

### E17-005 | Not a Patient | 1881-06

A physician acquaintance asked whether I thought the attachments of men such as Reiss could be described as a nervous disorder. Reiss himself was absent. I refused the premise that his life could be converted into a case study merely because he was not there to interrupt us. Reuter asked, reasonably, whether my objections were scientific or personal. I said that a diagnosis required a defined pathological process, not an offended audience. He brought several contemporary works on the matter; I read them and found the authors capable of observing great detail while committing themselves prematurely to explanation. I did not therefore conclude that all men who desired men resembled one another, still less that each man must live by a single new label. That evening I attempted a study of the Queen's appetite and found her unusually indifferent to my arrangement. I recorded the result without inventing a syndrome of disobedience. I congratulate myself, perhaps extravagantly, on having learned at least one lesson about the difference between behavior and disease.

### E07-001 | The Second Pendulum | 1881-07

My new timepiece had behaved impeccably until I carried it from Faber's workshop to a lodging whose walls were wet with August heat. The needle drifted by a small but intolerable amount. I returned to Prague with the instrument wrapped in a towel and my indignation left altogether uncovered. Faber compared it with his shop regulator, then put both beneath a bell and waited. They disagreed in a different direction. He refused to call either perfect, to my profound irritation. I proposed that a reliable instrument ought to survive a change of climate. He replied that I was describing a saint and that his profession dealt chiefly in metal. I paid for a second timing device and carried away the two together. Their differences became a column in the blue ledger. It was cheaper than certainty, though not by very much.

### E20-011 | Seven Years for a Wrong Question | 1881-08

Voss helped tie the notebooks of a seven-year series into three bundles. Every book carried some variation of the same idealized profile. I had improved the cheek, corrected the skull, and persuaded myself at least twice that the next vessel would contain the desired result. None had survived long enough to justify the final drawing. Voss asked what question the experiment had been intended to answer. I replied that form could be made to obey the intellect. She pointed out that I had answered a different question, namely how many failures one man would tolerate before improving his records. We ate sour apples while I wrote SERIES CLOSED. I did not write HYPOTHESIS FALSE. The distinction may be vanity, but it is also exact. A failure to obtain proof is not always proof of failure.

### E07-002 | A Parcel Marked Upright | 1881-10

Faber sent an exquisitely packed regulating spring, its crate painted with arrows pointing toward heaven. The railway company delivered it upside down. Lenz opened the box without comment and found the spring unharmed but the enclosing gauge dislodged. I wrote a complaint so elegant that it almost compensated for the journey. Lenz kept the crate, saying he could improve upon it, and returned the spring to its mount before supper. A week later he sent Faber a diagram of the improved packing wedges. Faber adopted them and wrote me a note of thanks for bringing the two workshops into correspondence. I had done nothing of the sort deliberately. The letter addressed itself to me, however, and I saw no reason to quarrel with the honor. The arrows remained visible on our storeroom wall long after their purpose had been exhausted.

### E04-008 | The Letter Recovered from a Fire | 1881-12

A chimney fire damaged Kappel's lodging and scorched the corner of the packet containing my early letters. He brought it to the tower without apology. I expected him to ask that I receive the papers back permanently. Instead he asked which should be copied first. We spent two evenings sorting his rescued books by the strength of their bindings and the value of their marginal notes. He spoke of retirement without complaint, as though a man might leave the guardianship of a library and remain himself. I was unsettled by how readily he described the change. Before going home he said that I should someday find another keeper for the blue ledger. I promised to consider it and did not. It was not his worst prediction of me.

### E23-006 | The Lever That Remembered | 1881-12

The brass lever had once responded cleanly to the slightest pressure. Now it caught at an angle and stained the thumb with grease. Lenz proposed a new fitting. I rejected it for want of funds, then discovered that the worn motion repeated more faithfully than the newer apparatus beside it. We measured the deviation and marked the handle with a black line. After that I could feel the small resistance through the glove before reading the scale. The instrument had acquired a character. Lenz called this the ordinary life of machinery. I objected to such undisciplined personification while returning to the same handle each morning. When the leather grip finally split, I had him replace the leather and preserve the metal. He shook his head and did exactly as I requested, which was, I believe, the first time he obeyed an instruction without argument.

### E14-016 | Two Kinds of Silence | 1882-03

Leonie and Voss met in my sitting room after a delay on the railway stranded them both until evening. I expected them to discuss my eccentricities, the one subject on which women of different professions sometimes discovered an alarming common interest. Instead they talked of a musician whose hearing had failed, of the price of lamp oil, and of a book whose illustrations were printed backward. I introduced an observation concerning the Queen. Voss asked for the measurements; Leonie asked whether the Queen had agreed to be watched. They pursued separate questions so persistently that I found myself having to answer both. Later, during a long silence, Leonie threaded a needle while Voss read a botanical account. I attempted an epigram about the refinement of intellectual company. Neither looked up. To my surprise I found the silence tolerable. Indeed I read for another hour without attempting to improve it. I have never accepted the sentimental doctrine that one needs company. I admit only that not all company is an interruption.

### E07-003 | A Bacillus at Breakfast | 1882-04

Reuter laid a Berlin report beside the marmalade and spoke with uncommon animation of Koch's announcement concerning tuberculosis. The decisive thing, he said, was not the beauty of the organism but its demonstrable association with disease. I admired the discipline of the claim and objected to his pleasure in rendering a great mystery small enough to stain upon a slide. Voss observed that I had made my own living creatures small enough to stand in glass. There are occasions when a woman should be made to pay for her analogies. I purchased Reuter's breakfast instead. That afternoon he showed me a row of ordinary hospital notes whose exact dates mattered more than their eloquence. I had expected him to praise discovery. Instead he praised the clerks who made it possible to determine what had actually happened. I found the sentiment revoltingly sound.

### E20-012 | An Invoice for Mercy | 1882-04

A patron asked to see a figure whose appearance I had described as nearly complete. It possessed an irregular shoulder which no careful lighting would disguise, but its responses remained remarkable. The patron offered to pay for its replacement if I would discard it and show him a prettier version. I informed him that I was not a manufacturer of dolls. After he departed Clara brought me the bill for a sounder vessel, a sum I could ill afford. I paid it by sacrificing a fine wool coat to the second-hand dealer. I was not acting from tenderness, I assured her. The cost of producing a responsive being was simply too great to throw away on account of a shoulder. She asked whether I would give the same answer if the figure were hideous. I told her to bring the invoice rather than philosophy.

### E04-009 | A Patient Walks Home | 1882-06

Reuter introduced a convalescent who came with a new hat, a bad cough, and complaints about his landlord. I pressed him to describe the period when his physicians had believed his life almost extinguished. He spoke instead of waking to discover the curtains had been changed and the rent was due. At last he asked whether I desired a more interesting memory. I told him I desired an accurate one. That ended our usefulness to one another. Reuter and I walked to a bakery, where the man had mentioned he could no longer afford his usual bread. I sent him a loaf by the porter and left no name. Reuter noticed, naturally. He had the irritating habit of noticing both what I did and what I pretended not to have done.

### E23-007 | A Lesson in the Spoiled Plate | 1882-06

A plate that should have remained clear acquired a milky bloom by morning. I was prepared to interpret it as an unanticipated phase of growth. Emil sniffed the rim and asked when the window had last been opened. The room was full of summer flies. I had omitted the cover for a moment while adjusting a lamp. It would have been a simple matter to photograph the bloom and discuss it as evidence that my formula was unusually vigorous. Instead I labeled it 'intrusion suspected' and placed it beside the next clean trial. A visitor later admired the clouded plate above all the others. I let him admire it until he had finished talking. The pleasure I took in the ensuing correction was perhaps not scientific. Emil remarked that I had, for once, resisted a magnificent error. He was unwise enough to add 'for once.'

### E07-004 | A Specimen from No One | 1882-09

A parcel of marsh plants arrived without a collector's name. Voss refused to use it in a comparative series because she could not establish where or when it had been gathered. I argued that a plant possesses an anatomy irrespective of its shipping label. She answered that anatomy was not the only thing I proposed to infer. We dried one sample and kept another growing, giving the parcel a new number but no invented origin. Weeks later a shipping receipt surfaced; the plants came not from the coastal marsh indicated on the crate but from a garden inland. My hypothesis concerning salt tolerance became ridiculous. I was pleased to have discovered the error before I announced it, and less pleased to owe this discretion to Voss. I kept the false label where I would see it whenever I opened the drawer.

### E04-010 | The Queen and the Stranger | 1882-11

The Queen began approaching Voss whenever she entered the room. She had once shown the same inclination toward Clara; I had assumed it would eventually extend to the person who had made and sustained her. Evidently monarchy possesses no instinct for gratitude. I changed the hours of Voss's visits, rotated the vessel, checked the light, and still the little ingrate moved toward the newcomer. Voss said I was treating her as a student selecting a favored professor. I said a professor at least provides the education. Clara laughed so heartily that I nearly dismissed them both. I recorded every approach thereafter, including those occasions when the Queen came to my side, a detail which I confess gave the table a more agreeable appearance.

### E23-008 | The Letter Sent to the Wrong Address | 1882-12

A package returned with my invented surname struck through and 'unknown' stamped beneath it. I had devised the address to protect the laboratory from journalists and creditors. Unfortunately a correspondent whose instruments I respected had enclosed three pages of observations and declined to risk a second dispatch. Mathilde arranged a forwarding place through her shop, requiring me to sign a written authorization in my proper name. I felt the loss of an elegant disguise more keenly than the arrival of the pages. The data contradicted an assumption I had defended at some expense. I worked through the figures by candlelight while Mathilde sorted invoices downstairs. On returning the pages I thanked the correspondent for his diligence but not Mathilde for hers. She charged me for forwarding; I paid at once, which was as near gratitude as the circumstances required.

### E07-005 | The Man with Three Watches | 1883-02

Faber's apprentice Pavel wore three watches, none of which agreed, because one had belonged to his brother and two had belonged to customers who never returned. The sentimental one kept the worst time. Pavel nonetheless reached for it whenever a whistle sounded in the street. Faber called that habit irrational. I replied that its purpose might have nothing to do with hours. The young man showed us that the back of the watch contained a scratched date. He would not explain it. I refrained from asking, which Faber regarded as an ominous novelty. Later Pavel designed a spring clip for one of my instruments and refused payment in cash, asking instead for a letter attesting to his work. I supplied the letter. Its praise was exact, its grammar impeccable, and its final sentence copied from none of my former recommendations.

### E17-006 | A Page Torn from the Programme | 1883-02

Leonie sent me a theatre programme annotated with a request that I attend a performance by two visiting musicians. Reiss met me at the side entrance. Inside, one musician played too rapidly and the other watched him with a concentration I recognized. During the interval Reiss asked whether I was enjoying myself. I said that the second player could have selected a stronger partner. He suggested that musical partnership was not always chosen by a committee of critics. On the reverse of the programme Leonie had sketched an absurd crown and written, 'Try staying until the end.' I stayed. The musicians made several errors together and recovered without a signal from the audience. Afterward Reiss invited me for a late supper with friends. I declined, citing the state of the laboratory, though the experiment could have been abandoned without loss. On the walk home I found that I could reproduce both players' mistakes. I could not reproduce the ordinary courage with which they had continued.

### E04-011 | Clara's Second Ledger | 1883-03

Clara kept a second ledger, not of my experiments but of prescriptions dispensed to people who could not pay. A merchant offered to buy the debts. I calculated what she stood to recover and urged her to accept. She declined because she knew the man's methods and apparently considered knowledge of character more valuable than ready coin. Three months later he was accused of extortionate collections. She took no pleasure in reminding me. She merely asked me to mend the cracked stool beside her till. I repaired it badly, was made to repair it again, and watched her account for medicines, customers, and favors that no bank would ever recognize as assets. Her shop was an organization of uncommon delicacy. I resolved not to call it a laboratory in her hearing; she would have charged me rent.

### E23-009 | The Winter Memory in Warm Weather | 1883-05

The day was hot enough to soften the leather binding of my notebook. The Queen withdrew from her chamber's exposed side as she had during the bitter winter two years before. I announced a hereditary disposition toward melancholy. Voss examined what had changed and found a folded cloth I had used to insulate the glass during that earlier season. We removed it. The Queen moved toward the light again. This did not prove she remembered a winter; it proved my grand theory of seasonal temperament was an unreliable substitute for looking beneath the jar. We repeated the trial after supper with the cloth arranged differently. I drank a little pear brandy and made notes that became less elegant as the evening wore on. Voss wrote the temperatures in her own hand, an affront I had invited by misplacing the thermometer twice.

### E14-017 | A Glass of Water in the Tomb | 1883-06

Georg fell ill while checking the old crypt's drains, and Matthias fetched me because I happened to be nearer than Reuter. I found Georg sitting on a step, pale and furious that his cousin had summoned help. His breathing was shallow, and the summer heat had made the stone chamber oppressive. I carried water and helped him to the gatehouse while Matthias sent for the physician. There was no experiment in the event, nor any useful speculation about the boundary between life and death. Georg recovered after several days and later accused me of looking more frightened than he had felt. I informed him that his recollection was medically unreliable. The next time I brought food to the crypt, I included a large flask of water and refused a cigar until we had both drunk from it. Matthias observed the change. I said the cemetery was simply a poor place to faint. Nothing in this explanation was false, which is not the same as saying it was complete.

### E04-012 | The Room in Vienna | 1883-10

In Vienna, Voss secured us entry to a laboratory where my name would have closed the door. She introduced me as a private investigator, which was quite true and wounded my pride only slightly. The director showed us preserved cultures whose apparent activity depended upon the hour at which they were examined. We spent two days quarreling over development and persistence. Rainwater came through the lodging-house ceiling and erased the tables I had spread across my desk. Voss produced a pocket copy. I told her she had the instincts of a clerk, and she replied that they were especially useful in the company of philosophers. We reconstructed the figures over disagreeable coffee. I bought her a better notebook afterward, without presenting it as tribute. She returned it filled with objections and two unusually useful diagrams.

### E07-006 | The Color of the Night | 1883-11

The sky had acquired an evening tint so peculiar that the villagers speculated freely about fires, storms, and judgment. Voss made sketches of the changing colors, while Clara brought a small kettle upstairs and asked whether the spectacle could be enjoyed without a lecture. I did not consider this a serious question. We watched from the broken parapet until the air grew cold. I proposed three possible causes, each with admirable disadvantages. The next morning an apprentice's watercolor arrived from Prague showing a similar crimson streak. We kept our local drawing beside it, dates carefully written. I had expected an apparition worthy of private interpretation and found a phenomenon distributed across the ordinary sky. I was disappointed only in the monopoly. Clara said the kettle had boiled dry. That, at least, had been entirely our own failure.

### E20-013 | The Perfect Thing That Choked | 1883-11

The chamber was perfect. Its seams vanished beneath a polished rim, its surface held not one visible scratch, and its occupant seemed for a time to sleep in a little palace. Then the signs of distress appeared. Reuter pointed to the airless stillness within the vessel and asked whether I had made a tomb rather than a home. I ordered it opened. The repair spoiled the symmetry of the lid, and a narrow new joint caught the eye like a scar. The creature continued to live. I wrote a full page defending the engineering principles of the earlier seal, and a single sentence recording its replacement. Reuter read both and said nothing. Years afterward I could still see the offending joint before I saw the occupant whose life had made it necessary.

### E23-010 | A Visitor Who Wanted Certainty | 1883-11

An instrument student at the Munich reading room studied the section drawing beside my elbow and asked whether the side joint was visible from the angle I had chosen. I told him that the drawing described a principle, not a carpenter's bill. He offered to turn the book so we might inspect its plates together. He smelled faintly of lamp oil and the leather used in repairing instruments. We found a missing dimension, but I refused to acknowledge that it invalidated the elegance of the figure. He copied it for me on a slip I folded and put away without asking his surname. Later I attempted to identify the stranger by his annotations and discovered that I had never asked his name. Memory is capable of excellent stage management; it reliably makes the people one disregarded seem more important in retrospect.

### E07-007 | The Image Behind the Glass | 1883-12

Mathilde asked whether her new illustrated circular could include a wood engraving of the Queen. I wanted a splendid portrait; Voss wanted a scale bar; Mathilde wanted a block that would print. We achieved none of these at first. The engraver made the Queen taller than the King and furnished her with a crown she had never worn. I rejected the block for scientific reasons, though it was admittedly magnificent. Mathilde had a second cut made from a plain drawing by Lenz, with the glass walls included and a scale beside the vessel. The result lacked drama. It also showed what could actually be seen. I kept the first block wrapped in brown paper. There are falsehoods too aesthetically competent to deserve destruction, provided no one mistakes them for observations.

### E07-008 | The Envelope that Stayed Sealed | 1884-03

Voss asked me to post a letter addressed to her mother. It was sealed in pale green wax and lay beneath the notebook in which I had just drafted a conjecture concerning the Mermaid. At the station I realized the notebook was missing and went back along the platform searching beneath benches. The train had gone before I found it beneath a discarded timetable. The green envelope was still in my breast pocket. I sent it by the next post, then told Voss what had happened, expecting a rebuke about the delay. She asked first whether my notebook had survived. I was oddly irritated. Her letter had become more important to me than my pages during the search, and she had managed to reverse our declared priorities without raising her voice. I never opened the letter, though she had left it in my possession long enough for anyone less trustworthy to do so.

### E04-013 | A Week of Silence | 1884-05

Voss ceased answering my letters. I entertained illness, displeasure, and intellectual treachery in that order, although I admit the last possibility received a disproportionately handsome amount of attention. In June she sent pressed leaves and a note: her mother's sight was failing, and she had been occupied at home. My reply began by regretting her distress and continued, out of habit, into a discussion of the Mermaid's seasonal movements. I cut the scientific pages and posted the remainder. Voss later wrote that she would have welcomed the ordinary account of my work. It would have been a familiar thing, she said, amid unfamiliar troubles. I kept the rejected pages, which still smelled faintly of sealing wax. There are occasions when an observation offered at the wrong moment is an intrusion, and others when silence is the worse presumption. I had managed to select neither with confidence.

### E20-014 | The Portrait That Omitted a Scar | 1884-06

Mathilde brought a photograph of the chamber whose lid we had altered. The printer had removed the awkward joint, producing a magnificent object that no longer existed. For several minutes I preferred the corrected likeness to the original. Lenz asked why the vessel in the photograph looked cleaner than the one on my shelf. I told him because a photograph was a statement of essentials, not an inventory of scratches. Then I looked again at the absence of the joint and remembered the creature's labored movement. I asked Mathilde to restore the mark before the image circulated. She looked at me as though I had proposed printing a confession. Perhaps I had. I nonetheless made her shift the shadow so that the repair could be seen without making the entire chamber look like an accident.

### E23-011 | The Clockmaker's Dinner | 1884-06

Novak had promised to inspect the travelling mechanism at the inn. He arrived late, limping, and spoke of his brother's long illness while the soup congealed between us. Voss listened. I checked the time twice and could not resist opening the instrument case. Novak closed it without raising his voice. We spent an hour discussing doctors, rail connections, and the indignity of standing in a corridor for news. Only then did he examine the gears. He worked in silence, his fingers black with oil, and corrected an error I had been concealing behind an elaborate adjustment. The following morning the mechanism kept time better than before. I have never established whether the improvement depended upon my patient listening. It is an experiment I shall not repeat deliberately. His brother recovered somewhat, which I learned from a postcard months later.

### E07-009 | The Garden with No Gate | 1884-08

Pavel visited Vienna carrying a replacement spring and insisted upon delivering it personally. Voss invited us into her family's narrow garden, where her mother sat beneath an apple tree sorting old labels by touch. The gate had been removed for repair; a stray dog wandered in and made off with the apprentice's lunch. He pursued it through the adjoining orchard. Her mother asked me how a professor of philosophy came to spend his days counting movements in miniature glass chambers. I gave the short version, which lasted seventeen minutes. She listened, then asked whether the creatures ever tired of being counted. I informed her that fatigue was a variable under investigation. She nodded as though I had answered the question. Later she returned a missing label to its precise place without sight enough to read it. I borrowed no philosophical conclusion from that modest feat. I remembered it nevertheless.

### E14-018 | The Box with a New Lock | 1884-10

Leonie sent a small wooden box with a new brass lock, made from a theatre property she no longer needed. Inside lay the first blue strip of cloth in which she had mounted the pin from my coat, along with three receipts and a pencil note: You are entrusted with more ordinary history than you suppose. I disliked the sentiment and admired the box. Voss suggested that the receipts be stored with my correspondence rather than among the instruments. I said that receipts could furnish excellent evidence of time and place. She pointed out that I had argued the opposite when asked to preserve the cost of a journey we shared. I placed the box on the third shelf, where neither woman would see it each time she entered. It remained within arm's reach of my desk. The key was so small I feared losing it and tied it to a red thread. Visitors often mistook the box for a repository of scientific secrets. I did nothing to correct them. Its contents were more embarrassing.

### E07-010 | The Unauthorized Abstract | 1884-11

A society circular repeated an abstract of my homunculus report under another man's name. It described the Queen as an ingenious mechanical figure, thus committing plagiarism and misunderstanding in the same sentence. Mathilde found the offending issue before I did and brought it to the tower with a pot of red ink. I wished to prosecute the author, publish the correction, and perhaps have him educated by force. Voss insisted that we first establish where his account had originated. The trail led to an intermediary who had copied only our illustration caption, omitting the qualifications attached to the drawing. I sent a correction limited to the false attribution and the mechanical claim. The culprit returned no apology. Mathilde printed our original caption in larger type, though it pleased her far less than a public duel would have done.

### E17-007 | No Properly Named Relation | 1884-11

Reiss wished to leave the region for a post in Switzerland. Mathilde prepared a bill for the pamphlets we had printed years before, although she knew he had no intention of settling it that day. He asked whether I would write a recommendation. I pointed out that I had been removed from a faculty and my authority was consequently of questionable value. He said my judgment had not been formally dismissed with me. I wrote the letter without mentioning his private life, which was none of the receiving institution's concern. Reiss looked at the seal and asked whether we had been friends. I said that friendship was a dangerously elastic word, stretched over acquaintances, debtors, conspirators and people who shared an inconvenient table. He said he would take that as yes. I did not correct him. His last letter from abroad described snow on the window frame and an unusually good class of students. The handwriting was smaller than before. Mathilde left his unpaid bill in the drawer rather than sending it after him.

### E04-014 | The Devil's Reflection Refused | 1884-12

The Devil refused his mirror one December evening. I had promised a demonstration to two friends, and the creature chose instead to sit with his back toward us, exhibiting a most unscientific talent for embarrassment. The King was unusually quiet, the Ballerina would not answer her music, and the chamber had grown cold. We restored the heat. The next day the Devil resumed his devotions before the glass. I wrote down every condition I could identify and refused to credit the warmth alone. Clara observed that I had been trying to summon yesterday's behavior from today's creature. An objection I had already entered in the ledger, though expressed by her with far less elegance. I changed my heading from 'obstinacy' to 'altered conditions.' I continued to call him the Devil. It suited us both.

### E23-012 | The Sale of an Unlovely Drawing | 1884-12

The patron selected the finest drawing, naturally, a specimen with a serene face and impossible hands. Mathilde pointed to a rough plate showing the same experiment before I had improved the proportions on paper. It bore a stain, three pencilled corrections, and an honest note that the subject had not survived the final interval. She proposed printing both. I accused her of conducting a campaign against beauty, a charge she did not deny. The pamphlet appeared with the damaged plate facing the ideal one. Two letters came asking about the stain, for it obscured an important measurement. Nobody inquired about the serene face. For a week I claimed the printing was an outrageous concession to vulgar tastes. I then ordered more copies. A flawed image had performed one of science's few truly decent offices: it had annoyed the right people into asking questions.

### E07-011 | A Bad Day for the King | 1885-01

Reuter arrived in January and found the King pressed beside the glass with one limb held at an alarming angle. I feared a return of the old injury. The physician did not accept the comparison and asked when the posture had begun. Lenz observed that a partition had shifted after the night's frost. We removed it and waited. The King moved slowly, then reached the Queen's side as though nothing extraordinary had occurred. Reuter urged continued observation rather than an heroic intervention. I accused him of practicing restraint as a form of vanity. He accepted the insult as if it had been a consultation fee. The next day I found myself hovering by the chamber more often than my own records prescribed. I moved my writing desk to improve the sightline, and told visitors it was for the better afternoon light.

### E15-001 | The Gentleman with the Unusual Question | 1885-02

Julian Adler introduced himself in Mathilde's shop as a dealer in old optical instruments. He had travelled from Vienna seeking a particular compound lens described in one of my obscure pamphlets. Most collectors wished to purchase an object and a story together. Adler asked why I had omitted the maker's name from the drawing. I explained that the instrument had been modified so extensively that attribution would have been misleading. He replied that modification did not excuse amnesia. Mathilde, who approved of this opinion, produced a proof bearing the original engraving. Adler examined its flawed lettering before its glass, which was enough to make him interesting. He wore a grey glove on the left hand despite the mild weather. I did not ask why. He proposed a visit to my tower. I refused. He offered instead to send a catalogue with comparative measurements. I accepted, since one can reject a person without refusing a useful instrument. The catalogue arrived promptly and included a correction to one of my dimensions. My reply was prompt as well, though I delayed posting it to avoid giving the wrong impression.

### E20-015 | The Failure Dinner | 1885-04

I had invited three people to hear a summary of the previous year's experiments, though the summary was chiefly an account of what had not happened. Clara arrived with onions, Reuter with a raincoat he would not remove, and Adler with a bottle he claimed was a more useful contribution than an opinion. I managed to burn the onions while lecturing on the difference between transient and durable responses. Reuter asked whether the subject on my shelf was still living. It was. Then, Clara declared, the year could scarcely be called a total failure. We ate on mismatched green plates and spoke for an hour about an innkeeper none of us liked. Afterward I found the ledger open to its page of disappointments and wrote SUPPER in the margin. It is the only result from that year I have never attempted to replicate precisely.

### E07-012 | The Inn's Second Guest | 1885-05

We stopped at an inn whose landlord mistook Voss for my daughter and quoted a single room. She corrected the relationship without looking at me. I was tempted to improve his education in several directions, but she had already secured two rooms and a place for our specimens beside the cold cellar wall. At supper a stranger asked whether our crates contained explosives. I told him they contained something more offensive to the complacent. Voss said they were plants. He lost interest. In the morning one crate had been moved to make way for the inn's apples. Our records showed no damage, yet Voss insisted upon recording the interruption. I admired her exactitude and resented how effectively a simple answer had protected our work. We travelled on in silence until she produced a pear stolen, she said, from the breakfast tray. I ate half and said nothing of theft.

### E15-002 | A Catalogue Without the Owner | 1885-06

Adler arranged for a damaged microscope stand to be exhibited at a sale in Vienna. He sent me a catalogue annotated with red circles around its faults. Voss, travelling to the city on botanical business, volunteered to inspect it. She reported that the maker's initials had been removed, not by age but with a file. I telegraphed Adler to refrain from bidding. He bought the stand nevertheless, at a price I considered unreasonable, and wrote that he wanted to discover why someone had erased a name. I lectured him by return post upon the difference between scholarship and collecting grievances. Three weeks later he found an invoice proving that the stand had belonged to a university collection disposed of without authority. He returned it to the institution and lost money. I expected him to congratulate himself extravagantly. Instead he asked whether I wished to see the invoice for my own records. I did. The hand that edits a name from an object, I wrote in the ledger, usually expects the object to remain silent. Adler had made a bad purchase and an excellent argument.

### E04-015 | The Salt Road to Trieste | 1885-08

Voss took me to Trieste in pursuit of marine preparations. The harbor had the smell of fish, wet rope, and commercial fraud. Our ordered specimens had been delivered to the wrong warehouse, where several had already spoiled. I questioned the shipping clerk until he wished to drown us both. The packing case Lenz had devised preserved what remained, and we returned with less material but rather better records than we had expected. Voss objected to the expense of pursuing such scant results. I replied that the cost of ignorance had never been reduced by remaining at home. This was a fine sentence and did not pay the invoices. The Mermaid became more active soon afterward. I attributed nothing to the journey without evidence, but I took a private satisfaction in the coincidence. One may enjoy a result before one understands it.

### E18-001 | An Instrument and Its Keeper | 1885-09

Adler returned to Mathilde's shop with the disputed lens and a scrap of cloth protecting its rim. He had brought its maker's mark into better light by cleaning away a century of accumulated polish. I praised the work, then questioned his method with enough severity to suggest the result had injured me. Julian refused to take offense. He placed the lens in my hand and told me to examine the new reflection rather than his character. I watched him pack his tools, arranging each in a leather sleeve cut precisely to its size. He had a scar along one knuckle; it appeared when he bent his fingers around the case latch. I asked about it. He said the workshop had been careless with a press. I found the scar more difficult to disregard than the instrument. Mathilde later said that I had become uncommonly attentive to the condition of other people's hands. I informed her that a craftsman's hands were an essential part of his apparatus, which was both accurate and not the entire explanation.

### E07-013 | The Apprentice's Correction | 1885-11

Pavel sent a new gauge with a note explaining that Faber's former design concealed a small lag at the return stroke. I assumed the young man had exaggerated a peculiarity to promote his invention. In Prague I tried the device beside its predecessor. The difference was real and, under one familiar condition, sufficient to invalidate a page of my old measurements. Faber observed my expression with a craftsman's detestable sympathy. I asked Pavel why he had not told me sooner. He answered that he had only just found a way to prove it. I bought the improved gauge and commissioned two more. His brother's watch still hung beside the window, its hands motionless. The lad no longer wore the other two. I had come to challenge his competence and left having increased my dependence upon it.

### E23-013 | The Restorer's Second Visit | 1885-11

Julian Adler came to repair a delicate catch whose failure I had attributed to careless workmanship. He showed me the wear had begun on my side of the apparatus, where a misaligned weight dragged upon the fitting. I disputed the matter for nearly an hour. He did not rise to leave, nor flatter me by yielding. Instead he placed the removed part beneath the lamp and invited me to watch it catch at precisely the point I had insisted was sound. When he had finished, I found another instrument requiring inspection. Its fault was trifling. He saw that at once and repaired it anyway. I do not pretend this was an experiment in companionship. It was merely that his departure would have interrupted an argument in which I had not yet achieved a satisfactory conclusion. He came back the following month without an appointment.

### E20-016 | The Equation with a Smudge | 1885-12

Mathilde noticed a smudge over one figure in an old table. I had read it as a three. The line beneath suggested an eight. Five subsequent books contained calculations that assumed the first reading was correct. Voss expected an explosion, and I supplied one, directed chiefly at the quill manufacturer and the premature fading of cheap ink. Then we fetched the first notebook. The number was eight. I allowed Mathilde to strike the dependent pages from the active series, though she used a thin rule and blue pencil where I would have preferred dramatic black. For the next week I was unpleasant company. On the eighth day I resumed the experiment from the corrected figures. It remains among the more laborious successes of my career, and produced nothing worth exhibiting.

### E15-003 | The Mirror Held Too Long | 1886-01

I finally admitted Adler to the tower because he possessed an unusual mirror intended for optical calibration.
