Voss was present, which I considered sufficient protection against the excesses of a collector's enthusiasm. Julian held the mirror while I adjusted an illumination aperture. In the reflection I could see the three of us arranged with a solemnity that would have delighted a provincial portraitist: Voss calculating, Adler steadying the apparatus, and I in the center, evidently waiting for mankind to deserve the privilege of my explanation. I made some remark upon my own resemblance to a theatrical villain. Julian replied that villains were often permitted better lighting than their victims. We laughed, then returned to the measurements. When the trial ended he held the mirror in place an instant after I asked him to lower it. He seemed to be studying my face rather than the beam. It may have been curiosity about the apparatus; the two objects were unfortunately aligned. I asked him nothing. For the remainder of the evening I was uncharacteristically severe about reflected light.

### E20-017 | The Little Imperfect Library | 1886-01

Mathilde arranged the notebooks on the upper shelf while I dictated what I believed to be a definitive taxonomy of successful experiments. She stopped me at the first category and asked where the books on things which had not succeeded should go. Into the lower cabinet, I said, where they would not encourage visitors. She opened one and found the correction to the smudged calculation that had wasted our winter. We spent the afternoon sorting errors by optical distortion, instrument failure, mistaken response and unfortunate enthusiasm. The titles were appalling. The usefulness was undeniable. I retained my own index of achievements near the desk and gave Mathilde her unlovely library. There are mornings when I consult it before looking at my handsome work. I do not find this an improvement in my temperament.

### E04-016 | The Strange Case on the Train | 1886-02

Voss brought Stevenson's new *Strange Case of Dr Jekyll and Mr Hyde* into our train compartment. She thought I would recognize myself in the divided physician. I said I recognized a man who had confused a chemical hypothesis with a sermon and then allowed his worse nature the better wardrobe. She objected that the horror lay in discovering one's conduct was not governed by one's professed principles. That, I replied, was scarcely a supernatural discovery; it occurred daily in faculty meetings. Yet I borrowed the book and read it a second time at the tower. I marked the pages in which Jekyll imagined he could separate appetite from responsibility by giving it another name. I did not tell Voss which passages I had marked. Nor did I return the volume promptly.

### E21-001 | A Journal Named for a Riddle | 1886-03

In Munich a bookseller placed a new journal in my hands, its title taken from the creature that asks a question before it offers an answer. Sphinx was much too pleased with its own mystery for my taste, yet its pages included arguments about hypnotism and the soul which no professor of sound reputation would have admitted to reading. I bought the issue and a cheap notebook. Wernig asked whether I believed in spirits. I replied that belief was a lamentably economical substitute for research. In the train I marked claims supported by observations in blue, those supported by admiration for the claimant in red. The latter color soon dominated the page. I nevertheless ordered the next issue. An idea may be badly defended and still point toward a question worth asking.

### E07-014 | The Trial with No Audience | 1886-05

We arranged a controlled observation of the Devil's response to reflections, concealing the mirror among several equally bright objects. Mathilde drew the order. Voss recorded the timings. I stood back and performed the difficult feat of permitting two women to conduct an investigation in my own laboratory without narrating each motion. The Devil attended first to a polished spoon, then ignored the mirror altogether. The following day he approached the mirror before the spoon. I had expected a tidy assertion of recognition and received a most unhelpful variety of attention. Mathilde found the uncertainty excellent for the next edition of the witness book. I found it unsuitable for the next edition of my reputation. We printed the result. The Devil remained quite unconcerned with either of our publishing interests and spent the afternoon examining the hem of his little black cape.

### E18-002 | The Words in the Margin | 1886-06

A volume of German commentary on friendship arrived from Julian with two passages marked in pencil. The first concerned the intimacy of men whose work required perfect trust. The second, less conveniently, concerned the forms of affection which custom made difficult to describe. I placed it beneath a stack of botanical catalogues until Voss arrived to retrieve her papers. She noticed the volume and remarked that a man who collected such a quantity of print could hardly object when the printed world described him. I told her that literature routinely mistook strong collaboration for sentiment. She asked which passage had compelled the complaint. I sent Julian a technical objection, twenty lines long, to the editor's translation. He answered with an account of how he had repaired the book's broken spine and added, 'I did not send it for the editor.' I read the sentence after supper and then again in the morning. I replied without discussing the second passage. Some invitations are most revealing in the care one expends pretending not to recognize them.

### E21-002 | The Dream of the Door in Glass | 1886-07

I dreamed of a door set into a wall of clear glass. The handle was on the other side, and behind it stood the Queen, no taller than she was in her chamber, regarding my efforts to reach her without the least alarm. I could have broken the wall, yet in the dream I was terribly concerned that the fragments might injure her. I woke with a cramp in my hand and the odor of a guttering candle in my nose. The drawing I made before breakfast showed a door too narrow even for a child. I labelled it architectural nonsense and shut it inside the blue ledger. It returned months later with different furniture and the same wrong-sided handle. I have never called it prophecy. It was sufficiently irritating as a dream.

### E23-014 | The Medium's Wrong Name | 1886-08

At the salon a medium pronounced a name I had written on a folded card which no one, I believed, had opened. She misplaced one letter. An error of that sort ought to have disposed of the claim. Instead it unsettled me, for it was the error I made myself when writing the name hurriedly. The room smelled of hot velvet and smoking lamp wicks. The hostess held the cards while the company congratulated itself upon a revelation. I asked to see the original. It had vanished amid the customary confusion of chairs and sleeves. Voss advised recording precisely what had happened rather than announcing that we had witnessed proof or deception. I drafted both announcements before tearing them up. The name survives in my notebook with its wrong letter circled. I still cannot say whether I had written it incorrectly at the start.

### E04-017 | A Printed Accusation | 1886-09

An unsigned notice in a professional journal condemned investigators who mistook stimulated tissue for independent life. I recognized several of my old opponents' phrases and immediately suspected Heller. I composed a reply of surgical exactness and malicious pleasure. Reuter asked me to identify the sentence which named either me or my work. There was none. Voss suggested that ordinary stupidity circulates quite freely without requiring an organized conspiracy. I retained the reply, unsigned and unsent, and folded the offending notice around Reuter's penciled words: 'No evidence of identification.' I still believe the author a fool. I am less certain that he enjoyed the privilege of being my particular enemy. This uncertainty did not make his prose any less objectionable.

### E07-015 | The Word Monster | 1886-10

A bookseller preparing a Gothic display suggested selling our old pamphlet beside tales of men whose experiments made monsters. Mathilde considered the extra trade. Voss thought the juxtaposition misleading. I declared that a monster was usually an object the observer had refused to understand, a principle useful enough to sell a dozen pamphlets. Then the bookseller requested a drawing with larger teeth. I informed him that my subjects possessed exactly the teeth supplied by nature and my own ingenuity, and that neither had undertaken work for his advertisement. We rejected the arrangement. After he departed Mathilde observed that I had declined an audience I had long begged the world to provide. I corrected her: I had declined *his* audience. We sold six copies that month, fewer than he promised, and the Queen remained unillustrated in his window.

### E15-004 | Two Ways of Reading Jekyll | 1886-11

Stevenson's account of Jekyll and Hyde passed from Voss to Adler and back to me with three sets of notes in the margins. I found the transformation less interesting than the doctor's vanity in imagining that he could separate responsibility from appetite by assigning them different names. Adler said the real scandal was that Jekyll required society's respectability and therefore invented a second life to preserve it. I called that a sentimental defense of concealment. Voss asked why I had written 'a practical arrangement' beside the scene in which Jekyll prepares a private entrance. I explained that every laboratory requires proper access control. They exchanged a glance which I disliked. That night I inspected the locked doors of the tower. They kept thieves away and also allowed me to receive different visitors without obliging them to acknowledge one another. A man might arrange his rooms for convenience and be accused, on literary grounds, of arranging his soul. It is one of the hazards of permitting friends to read one's marginalia.

### E21-003 | The Lady of the Blue Curtain | 1887-01

Frieda Selke conducted her sittings behind a curtain of faded blue silk. There were orange peels on a saucer and a bell cord within easy reach of a chair. I noted both and expected very little. She declined to summon my father, whose memory would have made a tedious witness, but described a visitor's habit of holding his gloves in one hand. I had never met the visitor. Neither had she, so far as I could establish. The description proved accurate. I felt the violent pleasure of a hypothesis gaining an unexpected ally. I also saw Wernig exchange a folded note with the hostess before we sat down. I returned for another sitting, this time without providing my name. Frieda greeted me as though she had expected precisely that impertinence. I enjoyed her intelligence and distrust equally.

### E04-018 | The Photograph on the Sill | 1887-04

Lenz's daughter, now a young woman, had taken an interest in photography and persuaded him to bring a camera to the tower. I permitted a portrait of the building, not of its inhabitants. She asked me to stand beside the doorway so that the scale might be understood. In the resulting plate I appeared taller and thinner than I had thought possible. Clara said the camera was an excellent corrective to vanity. I replied that it had made my ears absurd. The young woman offered to try again, but I kept the first print. Years later, when strangers described me as devilish, I suspected they had seen a photograph rather than a demon. The picture remained on the blue ledger's shelf, face inward when I was working.

### E15-005 | A Violin Without an Audience | 1887-05

Leonie had agreed to repair an old violin case belonging to Adler. Its velvet lining had rotted, and a seam had split across the leather. We arrived together to collect it. Leonie asked Julian to play while she finished the stitching. He said he had not played before strangers for years. She answered that she was too occupied with the seam to count as an audience. He performed four minutes of a Bach movement, stopping twice when his injured left hand failed to find the position. I had never asked about the glove. The music was neither flawless nor pitiable. When he finished I offered a technical suggestion concerning the instrument's bridge. Julian said the bridge was fine. He showed me the stiffness in his fingers and asked whether I could recommend a physician who would not promise a miracle. I gave him Reuter's address, taking care to write it legibly. Leonie returned the case without charging him. He thanked her by name. The exchange seemed to embarrass neither of them. I went home thinking of the part of the music he had declined to attempt.

### E23-015 | The Afternoon that Did Not Belong to Me | 1887-05

Julian had invited me to his lodging with the understanding that I would bring no work. I smuggled a folded sketch inside my coat. He saw its corner before I had removed my gloves and took it from me without ceremony. He placed it beneath a coffee cup, where a ring of dark moisture spread across the paper. I almost objected. Instead we opened the window and listened to carts passing on wet cobbles. The blanket over his chair smelled of clean wool; his coffee had grown a thin skin, which he stirred back into the cup with unconcern. We spoke of his childhood aunt, whose portrait he disliked, and of the journey I had declined the previous autumn. I went away with no new result in the ledger. The stained sketch remained at his lodging. On my next visit I brought nothing at all and found the precaution strangely burdensome.

### E21-004 | The Bell that Answered Twice | 1887-06

At the second sitting the little bell answered two questions with exquisite timing. I was considering whether an intelligence had been pleased to hear my voice when Adler pointed out that Frieda's sleeve grazed the cord as she turned. We repeated the motion without questions. The bell sounded. Someone at the table accused us of destroying a sacred atmosphere, as though the atmosphere were the sole witness whose testimony mattered. Frieda's face grew still. She asked whether I had enjoyed the two answers before knowing their source. I confessed that I had, and she said I was therefore no less a customer than anyone else. I paid the agreed fee, declined the third sitting and kept the memory of those two lovely false replies. They were false, not worthless. There is a distinction.

### E07-016 | A Card Sent Back | 1887-09

Faber forwarded a note from Pavel announcing that he would leave Prague to establish a shop of his own. I drafted a reply congratulating him while recommending that he postpone the venture until he had learned something of the world's hostility to independent craftsmen. Voss read the letter and returned it with a single word written in the margin: 'Yours.' I removed the warning and signed a shorter card stating that I would continue to purchase his work if the standards remained high. In December his first independent instrument arrived, packed in wedges devised by Lenz, with a bill neither discounted nor apologetic. It worked. I paid within the week. The card came back months later because Pavel had used my signature as a reference when securing his premises. I kept the returned envelope, amused that a modest courtesy had acquired commercial consequences beyond my supervision.

### E04-019 | The Queen Does Not Perform | 1887-11

Voss brought two visitors who hoped my miniature court would perform for them like an expensive clock. The Queen sat still. The King made a deplorable spectacle of himself; the Archbishop opposed him as usual; the Ballerina stopped after a few bars. I reached toward the vessels to improve the exhibition. Clara touched my sleeve, and I withdrew my hand. There was no grandeur in the moment. The visitors left unimpressed, having paid nothing for the privilege of misunderstanding the most extraordinary beings in Bavaria. Voss called it a victory that the creatures had been permitted to disappoint us. I called it an unsuccessful demonstration. Nevertheless I did not rearrange the Queen the next morning. A compelled marvel is a very poor advertisement for independence, and I wanted independence to remain my discovery.

### E15-006 | An Invitation with Two Signatures | 1887-12

Adler invited me to Vienna for an exhibition of optical devices. At the bottom of the letter he added that I might stay at his rooms rather than incur a hotel bill. His housekeeper had room for a guest, he said, and the library was adequate. I drafted a refusal which treated hospitality as a covert attempt to examine my working notes. Voss read the draft and asked whether I believed my own accusation. I said that belief was not necessary for caution. She pointed out that I had accepted Julian's corrections, his mirror, and Reuter's advice concerning his hand, all without the surrender of a single secret. I destroyed the draft. My reply accepted the exhibition but proposed a hotel near the station. Adler answered that this was sensible and enclosed a map. The map was drawn by his hand rather than printed. I carried it for the journey and later unfolded it so often that the lines began to split. There is a difference between declining intimacy and refusing a kindness. I was more adept at the first.

### E15-007 | The Coat at the Station | 1888-02

Vienna greeted me with sleet and the smell of coal. Adler had brought a second coat because mine, he said, was manifestly designed for a city in which the weather respected academic seniority. I refused it, then accepted it when a gust entered the station platform and settled that question experimentally. We spent the morning among display cases whose makers had improved their lenses and concealed the compromises in their mounts. At lunch Julian spoke of a brother with whom he had not corresponded for twelve years. He told the story without asking mine, a courtesy I found disconcerting. I said little. He did not treat silence as a challenge to be overcome. When we parted he asked only that the coat be returned by post. I sent it with a list of corrections to the exhibition catalogue and a note advising him to replace its missing cuff button. The button had been missing when he lent it. In the parcel I enclosed a new one, wrapped separately so it would not appear that I had repaired his garment out of sentiment.

### E18-003 | A Room without Witnesses | 1888-04

I was in Vienna for three days and spent the second evening at Julian's rooms, where the stove made a noise like a distant carriage. He had set out simple bread, cold fish and a small bottle of wine. We disputed the merits of a German translation until the clock struck two. He took the glass from my hand before I spilled it on his notes. I put my hand over his, not to reclaim the glass. He remained still. I kissed him; he returned the kiss, and for once neither of us attempted to improve the moment by speaking. We stayed together that night. In the morning the bread had gone stale, my waistcoat was draped over the chair, and his injured hand required time before it could manage the buttons of his shirt. I fastened one for him. He asked whether I would stay another day. I said I had a train and work waiting. He did not ask me to defend my priorities. I gave him the train's departure time and, without being asked, the date I expected to return. That addition has survived in my memory longer than the argument which preceded it.

### E04-020 | A Death with an Address | 1888-06

Kappel died in June, having devoted the last of his strength to arranging books rather than composing farewells. His nephew delivered my old papers in a bundle tied with green tape. Inside lay the brass key I had surrendered years before, polished by handling and utterly useless to the lock it once opened. At the funeral someone introduced me as an acquaintance. I nearly corrected him with a list of the man's services to my work, then found the catalogue inadequate and held my tongue. Back at the tower I opened Kappel's last note. It concerned the copying of pages whose ink had begun to fade. Voss helped me sort them by candlelight. I set a second cup beside the ledger before remembering that the man who had always complained about my tea would not be arriving.

### E12-001 | The Brass Key after the Funeral | 1888-07

Kappel died in June. In July Voss brought a small box containing the brass key with which he had once opened the cabinet of rejected work. The lock had been replaced years before. She asked whether I wanted the key for my papers or whether she should return it to the estate. I said the object was obsolete. She laid it on the desk while we discussed cataloguing his surviving notes. After she left I tried the key against the tower drawers, an activity with no conceivable scientific value. It fitted none. I wrapped it in a square of paper and wrote his initials upon the outside, though the paper already bore his handwriting. For weeks I was impatient with people who used the word 'loss' in my presence. I did not lose Kappel through carelessness. The key remained in the upper drawer. I seldom open that drawer without checking that it is there.

### E21-005 | Mesmerism without a Master | 1888-09

Reuter brought an account of experiments in hypnotism, and we attempted a modest demonstration with an adult volunteer who had agreed to it. Nothing supernatural occurred. The sitter reported heaviness in her arms and a curious alteration in the sound of my voice, though she also admitted she had expected both. When she said she wished to stop, I was on the verge of asking for one more observation. Reuter closed the notebook and stood up. I resented the interruption, because the most interesting sensation had only just begun to be described. The volunteer rose, drank water and went home. I recorded the request and the abrupt ending without removing them from the table of results. It annoyed me that Reuter's decision had been scientifically better than my intention and morally less theatrical.

### E23-016 | The Instrument No One Could Inherit | 1888-10

After the funeral I found an old instrument hanging above the bench, its control handle worn smooth in a pattern made by a hand I would no longer encounter. The spring had become brittle. Lenz proposed replacing it and leaving the original in a box. I forbade the disassembly. Several days later Clara asked whether I intended to use the apparatus or worship it. She was being deliberately offensive. I had Lenz replace the spring and leave the handle intact. The first movement came with a click slightly different from the one I remembered. I disliked it. Nevertheless the readings were sound, and the restored instrument remained in service. When alone I sometimes put my thumb on the polished place where the earlier hand had rested. I did not enter this habit in the laboratory book.

### E18-004 | The Agreement We Did Not Sign | 1888-12

Julian proposed that we agree upon what our acquaintance meant. He was not asking for an oath, still less a public announcement, but he wished to know whether my arrivals and departures belonged to a shared arrangement or merely to my convenience. I reminded him that my research could not be suspended for the sake of domestic regularity. He said he had never asked it to be. I then accused him of expecting an affection that could be scheduled. This was an answer to a question he had not put. Finally he said that he wanted to be free to accept another companion without committing a breach of a promise I refused to name. The possibility annoyed me more than it had any right to do. I declared that he was entirely at liberty. I continued stirring my cold coffee after the sugar had dissolved. He said that freedom offered with visible resentment was still freedom, but hardly a generous gift. We parted with a handshake in the crowded room. I disliked his correctness most of all because it did not make him less desirable.

## The later years of research and intimacy

### E05-001 | The New Keeper | 1889-01

Kappel's papers occupied the spare chamber, and no amount of tidying restored their former keeper. Voss proposed placing a working copy of the blue ledger in Clara's dispensary. I protested that a shop was not an archive. Clara looked up at the damp stain on my ceiling and inquired whether a leaking ruin was one. We established a register of every transfer, complete with dates and signatures. I expected the process to waste a week. On its first morning we found a report on the Mermaid misfiled with instruments and an error in Kappel's last copy list. I would have preferred a loyal archivist to a bureaucracy; a bureaucracy, however, had the advantage of surviving a single man's illness. I disliked the admission sufficiently to insist that my own original ledger remain in the tower.

### E12-002 | The Friend at the End of the Table | 1889-03

Clara and Voss arranged Kappel's papers in the east chamber. They had divided his correspondence into subjects, dates, and matters which could not be reconciled by either. I objected to the third category as slovenly. Voss presented a letter in which Kappel described me as exacting but generous with time I did not think belonged to anyone else. Clara laughed at the accuracy of it. I demanded to know why he had not told me this while alive. She said he had tried repeatedly. We read no more of his personal observations that afternoon. Instead I asked to see his old catalogue, the one with corrections made in three shades of ink. I could recognize the years of his hand by the pressure of his pen. The two women left the third category in place. I never again insisted it be emptied merely to satisfy my need for a clean classification.

### E24-001 | The Familiar Invoice | 1889-04

Rosa set her battered tin measure on the table and announced that her knee would no longer permit the hill after heavy snow. She had brought a younger cousin to manage the cart. I protested the substitution as though a coal delivery were a university appointment. Then she produced her notebook. It held thirty years of weights, prices, missing payments and remarks in an irritable handwriting remarkably like my own. Beside one winter she had written 'Professor forgot to eat' and beside another 'Professor swore the Queen preferred the dark.' She knew more of the household's history than any published memoir. I spent an evening comparing her figures with mine and found that my accounts did not balance in two exceptionally expensive years. Rosa pointed to the repairs I had charged to the specimens instead of to myself. I paid what I owed her. I left her descriptions uncorrected.

### E15-008 | The Refusal I Had Prepared | 1889-05

Adler asked permission to describe one of my miniature figures in an exhibition catalogue. I had prepared a refusal involving the word desecration, which I was certain he would find memorable. Mathilde read his proposed description first. It was concise, accurate, and gave the creature no name. I objected that to omit the Queen's title was to strip her of individuality. Julian asked whether the title belonged to her or to me. That question had become a tiresome favorite among my friends, as though good questions improved with repeated use. I offered to supply a revised caption. He accepted, provided its claims remained observational. We spent two afternoons disputing eight lines. The final description mentioned my authorship, her voluntary choices, and the limits of what the display could prove. It was better than either of our drafts. The exhibition proceeded without the living Queen, using a drawing instead. I told everyone that I had refused a vulgar public spectacle. I did not mention that Adler had suggested the drawing before I did.

### E05-002 | A Visitor Who Asked About Pain | 1889-07

Reuter at last visited the tower. He passed over my homunculi's elegance without a compliment and watched the King push against the partition installed after his fall. Did the restriction distress him? I replied that it prevented another injury. Reuter observed that both statements might be true. He was too practiced a physician to expect a lecture to settle the question, and I was too practiced a philosopher to resist attempting one. Voss proposed recording the King's activity under different harmless arrangements. We did so, and the observations answered less than either of us wished. I altered the cleaning and maintenance schedule to minimize unnecessary interference. Reuter treated this as evidence of progress. I treated it as experimental housekeeping, which is a more respectable name for being influenced by an adversary.

### E12-003 | The Visitor's Second Glass | 1889-10

Reuter came with questions about fatigue in the small creatures and departed before supper. I had poured two glasses of gin, expecting that the argument would last longer. Voss arrived to collect notes and found me contemplating the untouched second glass. She asked whether I wished company. I told her I already possessed a rather demanding household behind glass. She sat anyway and spent half an hour reading quietly by the window while I annotated a table. We said almost nothing. When she rose to leave I realized I had reached the end of the table and begun writing in the margin. I offered her the second glass. She declined and asked for tea instead. The gin remained on the tray until morning. I had long said that conversation was the principal value of company. When Voss came the following Thursday, I had set out the teapot before I heard her on the stairs.

### E21-006 | The False Ancient Seal | 1889-12

Wernig sold me a folio said to preserve an early alchemical emblem, a figure curled within a closed vessel. It pleased me excessively. I spent a week comparing its circles with the compartments of my own chambers, until Mathilde noticed that the page numbering came from a modern edition. The emblem had been rearranged for a bookseller's plate. I could not prove the publisher's intention, but I could prove it was no untouched survival from an earlier century. I wrote the correction against the purchase price. The design still suggested a way to arrange observations by nested dependencies. I used that suggestion and refused afterward to describe it as ancient wisdom. Wernig offered to take the book back. I kept it, though I ceased to mention its pedigree over supper.

### E24-002 | The Keeper's Revised Paper | 1889-12

Clara arrived carrying brown parcels and a clear account of the morning's work. I gave her a page of instructions, arranged so precisely that any intelligent person ought to have been grateful. She asked why the north shutter must be closed before the last bell, why one chamber remained partly shaded, and whether she was forbidden to enter the upper room because of danger or vanity. My explanations became less satisfactory as the questions proceeded. I gave her the first three; the fourth concerned my private formulas and remained my affair. She wrote 'unexplained restriction' beside it. I did not dismiss her. By the following week she had made a small list of procedures that no longer served their stated purpose. I concealed my annoyance beneath the observation that a keeper with a mind of her own was a more expensive form of apparatus. She replied by requesting better paper.

### E12-004 | The Correction Not Sent | 1890-01

Voss sent a manuscript in which she challenged an interpretation of the Mermaid's responses to changing salinity. I drafted a reply demolishing four minor errors and avoiding the central objection. Mathilde arrived for corrected proofs, read the first page at my invitation, and asked why Voss would be persuaded by criticisms she had already conceded. I said scientific debate requires that all errors be examined. Mathilde pointed to the untouched final paragraph. I rewrote the reply overnight, conceded that Voss's explanation fit the available observations, and requested a new trial. In the morning I considered restoring the original opening, which was immeasurably more elegant. I posted the shorter letter. Voss replied with a proposed experiment rather than a defense. There are colleagues whose agreement I value because it signifies admiration. Her disagreement was valuable for the more inconvenient reason that it continued to produce work.

### E05-003 | The Metronome Comes Apart | 1890-04

The cracked metronome had followed me from student rooms to the tower without keeping satisfactory time for most of the journey. Its spring finally broke while I was clearing a shelf. Lenz offered to replace it, but I asked him instead to open the case so I could see where the metal had fatigued. We laid the pieces on green felt, and he showed me the tiny points where repeated strain had done its quiet work. I was tempted to draw a metaphor about men of science. Lenz advised me to purchase a new spring before delivering a sermon. We repaired it, but I never used it again for measurement. It remained on the shelf as a reminder of error corrected, not a shrine to error perpetuated.

### E21-007 | A Dream with Three Queens | 1890-06

The door returned. On its far side stood three Queens, identical in feature and entirely different in conduct. One knocked upon the glass. One looked away. The third held the handle and would not open it. I woke angry at having been excluded from a room I had apparently constructed myself. Over breakfast Clara remarked that I had spoiled the milk by leaving it too near the stove. Without quite intending to, I told her the dream. She said three creatures might not wish to be treated as one, however they had begun. I accused her of importing philosophy into a damaged night's sleep. She poured fresh milk, which proved wiser than arguing. I drew the three figures separately in the notebook and did not connect the drawing to an experiment until much later.

### E15-009 | The Letter Addressed Elsewhere | 1890-07

A letter from Adler came addressed to Voss at the tower rather than to me. I opened it before recognizing the handwriting, and the first sentence concerned a botanical instrument she had requested. The rest discussed his plan to travel farther east in search of a restorer. There was nothing in it that entitled me to a reply. I nevertheless told Voss that Julian's scheme was unsound and offered several corrections. She asked why I had read a letter bearing her name. I informed her that our papers were often delivered together. She said this was an explanation of opportunity, not of right. I returned the letter and did not speak of it again that day. The following morning I wrote Julian an unrelated note asking for a measurement of an old aperture. He answered, briefly, with the measurement and no invitation to continue. For several weeks the silence annoyed me. Eventually I understood that the annoyance proved something about my expectations, though not necessarily anything admirable. I stored the envelope with its damaged seal in a drawer marked for optical correspondence.

### E24-003 | The Metronome's Empty Beat | 1890-07

The Ballerina responded, as she had always done, to the phrase she preferred. I called it exquisite discrimination and played the passage again. Clara asked whether the creature stirred before the first note. I accused her of mistaking the movement of my hand for the movement inside the glass. She placed a felt pad beneath the mechanism and counted the beats aloud until I could bear the sound no longer. Then she stopped one beat before the accustomed phrase. The Ballerina moved. The room carried the stale fragrance of rosewater used by the former keeper; the little hammer hung above a note it never struck. I spent a fortnight examining whether anticipation survived when the familiar sequence was interrupted. It did sometimes, and sometimes did not. I have not found a more economical explanation than learning. I still play the whole passage when visitors come.

### E12-005 | The Things in My Pockets | 1890-08

Clara emptied the pockets of my coat before giving it to Lenz's wife for repair. Out came a broken measuring rule, a wax seal, two cigar ends, a folded shop bill, and a scrap on which the Queen's movements had been sketched in hurried figures. Clara regarded the collection as evidence that I was developing into a travelling rubbish cabinet. I told her it represented a portable scientific archive. The rule had once belonged to Kappel, the seal to Mathilde's early edition, and the bill carried Lenz's characteristic spelling of my name. I could have discarded all of them without compromising a single experiment. I put them back after the stitching was finished. At a later date I began using a small tin for the scraps, which was a concession to fabric rather than memory. My pockets have never been especially economical.

### E18-005 | A Letter That Was His | 1890-10

I had already offended Voss by opening a letter Julian addressed to her. My explanation about shared correspondence was ingenious and unconvincing. Julian wrote separately to say that Voss had asked for an instrument drawing and that he intended to treat her request without my supervision. The paper bore the same careful hand I knew from his catalogues, but no familiar flourish at the close. I began a reply accusing him of making a principle out of a clerical oversight. Instead I sent a short acknowledgment, and a second envelope containing the diagram I had withheld in case he should require it. Weeks later Voss returned from travel and found my desk cleared of the offending letters. She did not thank me. She said that trust between adults was not a cabinet to which one man issued the keys. I told her she had selected an elaborate metaphor. I had, however, ceased opening her post. Julian sent a technical correction the next month, and I read it without looking for an accusation between its measurements.

### E05-004 | The Woman in the Corridor | 1890-11

Voss sent for me while attending her mother in Vienna. She wished to ask a technical question about a botanical specimen, but the visit chiefly consisted of sitting in a corridor where cups were collected with a clatter at regular intervals. Her mother was growing blind, and the physicians had little to offer. I attempted to describe the difference between lost perception and lost understanding. Voss told me, very gently, that she was not conducting an inquiry. We sat without speaking. Before leaving I repaired the catch on the specimen box she had carried from home. She thanked me for coming. No result was published, and I remember the visit as one of the few occasions on which absence of useful speech was not a failure.

### E24-004 | An Anniversary Not My Own | 1890-12

Mathilde's dining room was full of paper flowers and an absurdly large cinnamon cake. I had brought a manuscript in a leather case, believing this to be the subject of her note. The gathering celebrated her mother's seventieth year. No one asked about my papers. I sat near the shutters, drank weak tea, and endured a child's account of a mechanical toy whose wheels would not remain attached. When the child asked my opinion, I examined the axle and suggested a repair. Mathilde took the case from beneath my chair and placed it by the door, where it remained all evening. I objected when I left, saying that she might at least have allowed me a glance at the proofs. She reminded me that she had invited a man, not a manuscript. I remember the cake rather well. I do not remember what I had intended to argue.

### E21-008 | The Unpaid Assistant of the Invisible | 1891-02

Frieda agreed to meet me at a coffee room where no curtains could assist an apparition. I asked for copies of her records and was prepared to pay. She produced one transcript, the record of my own questions, and refused to supply the names of other sitters. I called this unfortunate for science. She said it was fortunate for the people who paid her to be discreet. Then she asked why I had never published the names of everyone who helped me before my dismissal. I found the analogy impertinent and, after consideration, accurate enough to be inconvenient. I paid her for the transcript and left the other questions alone. It was not a conversion to spiritualism. It was an admission that even questionable practitioners may keep an obligation which a respectable man would violate.

### E15-010 | The Acknowledgment Without a Demand | 1891-03

Julian wrote to say that he had found the restorer he sought and intended to remain in Vienna for the year. Voss thought I might congratulate him. I composed a note offering advice upon the management of an instrument workshop, a subject on which I possessed opinions but no workshop. After reading it I tore it across the signature. My second letter contained three sentences: his search had produced a result, his catalogue description of the Queen remained useful, and I trusted he would find suitable work for his hands. That final remark was perilously personal. I sent it before I could replace it with a joke. The reply arrived a month later. He thanked me, said his left hand had improved somewhat, and enclosed a lens washer I had been unable to obtain locally. Nothing further was implied. I examined the washer for defects, found none, and put it to use. It was the most satisfactory correspondence we had exchanged in years, not least because neither party had been forced to explain himself.

### E05-005 | A New Name on the Packing Case | 1891-05

Anna Lenz, whom I had once seen asking clocks whether they knew the hour, had become competent with photographic plates and inventory records. She labelled a crate of instruments with a code that allowed each item to be matched to its drawing. I told her the system was unnecessarily elaborate. She opened the crate and identified a damaged component before I could discover that the wrong object had been packed. Her father watched me concede the point with open enjoyment. Anna asked whether I preferred to be addressed as Professor or Doctor. I said the former was more historically interesting and the latter presently more useful. She called me Doctor and charged for the improved packing. I paid without bargaining and remembered the child at the river.

### E12-006 | The Apprentice's Own Signature | 1891-06

Anna delivered a newly repaired joint accompanied by her father's bill. The glass was sound, but the invoice bore a set of alterations in a hand I did not recognize. I crossed them out and marked the page for Jakob's approval. Anna returned the bill the next morning with the same figures, now signed in full. She explained that she had made the joint and had no intention of being paid through someone else's reputation. I protested that I had known her since she was scarcely tall enough to reach the bench. She replied that neither of us ought to be charging for work done at that height. I paid the invoice and asked her to examine an old valve I had trusted for years. She condemned it in six minutes. I was proud of the Lenz workshop and resentful of its apparent intention to supply the world with competent critics of me.

### E24-005 | The Second Hand on the Page | 1891-08

A summer storm darkened the window while I recorded the Queen's refusal of a freshly decorated compartment. I described the refusal as sovereign disdain. Anna had watched from the other side and said the Queen entered the room twice before retreating after a flash of lightning. Voss, who arrived later, believed that the creature avoided a certain smell from the new varnish. All three interpretations were plausible. I wanted my conclusion, because it was the most becoming to its subject and creator alike. Instead we wrote separate accounts in violet ink, signing the margins. I warned them that multiplying witnesses did not necessarily multiply truth. Anna answered that my single witness had hardly done better. The notebook thickened with such competing voices over the next years. I disliked its appearance, but increasingly feared the neat books that lacked them.

### E12-007 | A Name Written Smaller | 1891-09

Anna proposed engraving her initials on the base of the new glasswork so that future repairs might be traced to the proper maker. I said manufacturers had survived for centuries without turning every joint into a monument. Voss produced one of my first blue ledgers and showed me where I had complained of unacknowledged work at twenty. I accused them both of conspiring against a harmless preference for visual cleanliness. Anna offered to make the mark small enough that only a repairer could see it. I agreed. Six weeks later the mark allowed us to identify which batch of joints had a slight internal defect. I wrote to Anna congratulating her on the usefulness of the mark and said nothing about our original disagreement. It had been settled, after all, by data. She returned the letter with the word 'naturally' pencilled beneath my signature.

### E21-009 | An Ethical Error without a Violation | 1891-10

Anna had prepared a flawless chamber for a trial intended to test how long a subject would tolerate an unpleasant change in light. The question had little value beyond satisfying my curiosity and proving that I could sustain control. Voss asked what I expected to learn that ordinary observation had not already shown. I began an impressive explanation and found it wanting halfway through. The little figure continued its daily movements while we argued. Finally I instructed Anna to return the chamber to storage. She asked whether she had made an error in its construction. Not at all, I told her. The apparatus was perfect. The question was vulgar. I resented having to distinguish between the two, and the distinction thereafter became one of my less advertised rules.

### E05-006 | A Night of Melted Snow | 1891-12

Snow slid from the tower roof and blocked the outer drain, sending water beneath the glass-room door. Clara arrived with towels; Anna arrived with a camera she had intended to use for an experiment and immediately put it aside. We carried every living vessel to the dry chamber. The Mermaid's jar was heavier than it looked, and I nearly dropped it when my foot slipped. Anna steadied my arm and told me to pay attention to the floor rather than the specimen. She was right. We lost several loose pages and a box of old labels but no creatures. The following week I insisted upon a new drainage channel. I paid for the work with money reserved for an optical experiment, and did not regret the exchange until the invoice arrived.

### E24-006 | A Gift Refused for the Right Reason | 1891-12

I presented Julian with an instrument restored so perfectly that even its former owner might have failed to recognize it. He admired the work and asked whether I expected him to take over the measurements I had been neglecting. I said I expected nothing. He opened the leather case and showed me the list of trials I had enclosed beneath the lid. Frost formed on his window while the lamp smoked. The silence that followed was one of those rare intervals during which my wit deserted me through no fault of the company. He closed the case and returned it. I carried the instrument home, furious with the ingratitude of a man who had apparently understood my intention too well. We exchanged ordinary letters afterward, without a ceremonial reconciliation. I still possess the unused instrument. Its workmanship remains flawless, a fact not entirely to its credit.

### E12-008 | A Cigar without an Audience | 1892-01

The winter evening was quiet enough that I heard the ticking of the damaged metronome in the next room. I lit a cigar after supper and read a reply from Voss disputing my projected dimensions for a larger living form. The letter was severe, precise, and accompanied by measurements I could not readily evade. I put it down, walked twice around the room, and allowed the cigar to burn out between my fingers. I was annoyed with Voss for being absent when I had finally constructed a response. I began a letter, abandoned it, and reopened hers. Near the end she had added that she would be in Vienna until March and hoped I would not neglect the little creatures while defending an impossible machine. I wrote back with the corrected dimensions first. Then, beneath my signature, I asked whether she would require anything from Ingolstadt. I did not include an explanation for the question.

### E24-007 | The Supper in the Wrong Room | 1892-03

I had grown accustomed to eating wherever the day's work released me: at the bench, in the crypt, once upon a crate of empty jars. Clara objected to my chicken in the upper room not because the place was unclean, but because I had forgotten that she and Anna were waiting below. I insisted that an important observation had detained me. The observation had been the King watching his reflection. She offered no counterargument and brought the food upstairs. The skin had cooled to a disagreeable firmness. We ate at the library table with candlewax collecting upon an old botanical catalogue. I declared a weekly meal with witnesses unnecessary. They continued it without seeking another declaration. There were evenings when I arranged my experiments to finish early. I have never been able to explain that scheduling choice as a scientific necessity.

### E15-011 | A Funeral Without a Sermon | 1892-04

Georg's younger brother died after a winter illness, and the family asked for no public ceremony beyond a short reading. Matthias delivered it in an unadorned voice. I stood near the path with my hat in my hand, intending to leave before anyone could mistake my attendance for a profession of belief. Georg stopped me by the gate and asked whether I thought anything survived death. I had prepared a careful answer about continuity of matter and the absence of evidence for a surviving person. Then I saw that he was not inviting a debate. I said I did not know. He nodded and returned to his family. On the walk home I resented the inadequacy of the answer because it was also accurate. I thought of my six little creations, each brought into being by a method I refused to publish, and recognized that none had answered Georg's question. The next time I visited the crypt I brought his favorite onions and left the theological discussion at the door.

### E05-007 | The Theory of a Larger Life | 1892-06

The enormous empty vessel still occupied the room it had been built too large to leave. Voss asked whether my desire for a full-sized creature was scientific or merely a protest against visitors who called my little people curiosities. I replied that the restriction of growth by scale was a legitimate problem and that her two explanations were not mutually exclusive. We drew separate plans: one for larger organization, one for observing the present creatures without interfering with them. The second required fewer resources and furnished the immediate work of a month. The first I locked away, not from shame but from the unpleasant knowledge that ambition is expensive before it becomes glorious. I examined the locked drawing every morning. A problem does not cease to exist because a sensible woman has supplied a cheaper one.

### E12-009 | The Parcel with No Instrument | 1892-08

A parcel came from Voss containing an old book and a packet of good coffee, but no measurements, specimens, or requests. I examined it twice for an accompanying problem. Clara said perhaps someone had simply sent me a present. I explained that scholars exchange tokens to maintain correspondence. She pointed out that Voss had already written twice that month. I placed the coffee in the kitchen and delayed opening it for several days, unwilling to make the gesture seem necessary. When I finally brewed it for Clara's visit, it proved remarkably good. I sent Voss a three-page note about an observation she had requested weeks earlier and appended one line about the coffee. Her next letter contained a small joke about my elaborate payment for a kindness. I retained the wrapping paper because it was excellent for protecting delicate specimens. That, at least, was the reason I supplied to anyone who noticed it.

### E18-006 | A Visit Not Entirely Scientific | 1892-08

Julian arrived unexpectedly with a small chest of instrument parts, and I nearly concealed my pleasure by accusing him of failing to announce the journey. He said he had already written; the letter was in my pocket. Leonie, who was visiting with a parcel of black cloth, greeted him as though she had long expected our paths to become indistinguishable. That evening Julian helped fit a shutter in the south room. I instructed him in the operation of the hinges and he reminded me that he had repaired more delicate machinery before breakfast. When Leonie left, she touched his sleeve and told him to insist upon supper before allowing me another demonstration. She did not treat him as a secret or a spectacle. The following morning I discovered that Julian had left a folded square of cloth beside my cup to protect the table from a leaking reagent bottle. I placed it there again after washing it. I had contrived no new discovery during his visit. Nevertheless the hours were not, by any sensible reckoning, lost.

### E05-008 | An Unanswerable Question of the Queen | 1892-10

The Queen began to grow still whenever we approached with certain instruments. Clara carrying fresh supplies produced no such effect. I called it anticipation. Voss asked whether odor, shape, or some regularity of our movements might suffice to explain it. We brought harmless objects to the bench and varied the observer, the order, and the light. The Queen was exquisitely uncooperative: responsive on one occasion, indifferent on another. I had made her, or at least had given the conditions in which she came to be; I had not instructed her to form expectations about me. If she had nevertheless acquired them, they might prove my deepest claim more decisively than any graceful limb. I was delighted by the thought and troubled by what those expectations might contain.

### E24-008 | The Glass that Could Not Grow | 1892-11

I had enlarged the chamber with extraordinary attention to proportion. Its supports were stronger, its seals finer, and its occupant no nearer to remaining viable beyond the familiar interval. The fourth failure was accompanied by a shattered rim that cut Lenz's finger. He wrapped it in clean linen and went home without waiting for my apology. Voss said the next attempt would reproduce the same ignorance at a greater cost. I replied that the scale of an error is sometimes what reveals its nature. She asked what new variable I proposed to change. I could name none. We suspended the series. For weeks I passed the large empty vessel and felt the peculiar insult of an instrument that had performed all its duties while the hypothesis collapsed. I did not dismantle it. A large failure occupies a room with much greater authority than a small one.

### E21-010 | The Face in the Dirty Window | 1892-12

I was climbing the eastern stair after midnight when a pale face appeared against the window, seeming to look inward from a height no man could have reached. I stopped with one hand on the latch. When the rain shifted, so did the features. There had been no visitor. Adler, arriving behind me, asked why I had gone so white. I said the stair was cold. He stood with me while we watched the face dissolve into streams of water and the faint reflection of my own lamp. Later I drew the pattern in the same book that held my glass-door dream. I knew perfectly well what I had seen. What troubled me was the interval in which I had not known. I did not give that interval the dignity of a ghost story.

### E15-012 | The Sale I Was Prepared to Force | 1893-02

An estate catalogue offered a rare regulator clock with a mechanism I believed could improve our timed observations. The seller insisted upon selling the whole lot, including several worthless cabinets and a portrait of a miserable-looking magistrate. I wrote a letter implying that the collection was likely to be exposed as fraudulent if he refused a private sale. Mathilde saw the draft beside the post tray and asked whether I possessed evidence for the threat. I did not. Anna proposed building the relevant regulator from modern parts at less than half the price. I disliked both objections. The letter went into the fire, and Anna built the apparatus. It performed imperfectly at first and required three corrections, all recorded under her name. I had been prepared to frighten a merchant into parting with property because I wanted an instrument quickly. The alternative demanded patience and recognized somebody else's skill. I still do not know whether the seller was dishonest. That uncertainty is fortunate for him. It is less flattering to me.

### E05-009 | The Letter I Did Send | 1893-03

I had drafted many letters to Henry in the years since his last reply, almost all unsent. This one was brief. I wrote that I remained concerned with living organization, that my smaller creations continued to survive, and that I hoped his own investigations had not brought him undue trouble. I enclosed no technical drawings. Voss examined the envelope, not the contents, and asked why I had waited so long. I told her that serious men should not burden one another with domestic sentiment. She said serious men were perfectly capable of deciding which letters to answer. I posted it myself. No reply came. I do not know whether he received it. I have been tempted to write an entire theory to explain that uncertainty, but it remains only a missing answer.

### E12-010 | The Envelope from Henry | 1893-04

I had sent Henry a letter in March after years of attempting to compose one that would be neither a plea nor an indictment. In April I caught myself listening for the postman's steps while pretending to measure the light at the window. Mathilde arrived with a proof and saw the addressed envelope lying beside a second sheet upon which I had begun writing another explanation. She asked whether the first letter had contained a question. It had contained several. She asked why I was attempting to answer them before their recipient had read them. I tore the second sheet in two and put the pieces in the stove. The postman came that afternoon with a receipt for chemicals and no reply. I was perfectly capable of continuing the experiment. I repeated one set of measurements needlessly. The figures did not improve merely because I was paying them extra attention.

### E24-009 | The Printed Marriage Notice | 1893-05

I came to the shop for proofs and saw a marriage announcement in the newspaper stacked for wrapping. It concerned a man I had once known rather better than the world would have approved, now described in three lines as a respectable husband. A crushed carnation lay on the counter and marked the paper with a thin red stain. Mathilde pretended to be absorbed in an invoice. I cut out the notice with her paper knife, folded it twice, and put it into my pocket. She said nothing and brought the proofs. I corrected every comma with unusual severity. That evening I filed the clipping between measurements of vessel pressure where no rational archivist would think to look for it. It has no scientific value. I have retained things of less value under that pretext for much longer.

### E08-001 | The Letter with Two Endings | 1893-06

The letter I had at last sent to Henry in March left behind two discarded endings. One asked him to write about the work. The other asked whether he still remembered our evenings disputing the shape of life over badly trimmed lamps. Voss read neither, but Mathilde, collecting papers for binding, discovered both beneath the blotter. She offered to destroy them. I declined. A man may reject a sentence without condemning it to death. Weeks passed without an answer. When another parcel arrived from the post, I went to the door with an eagerness sufficiently vulgar to annoy me. It contained an invoice for paper. I paid it late. Mathilde said I had begun handling every foreign envelope as though it were explosive. I instructed her to improve the postal system rather than my temperament.

### E21-011 | The Second Life of a Broken Jar | 1893-07

A repaired jar arrived with a scratch across one side. I was preparing a complaint when Anna observed that the mark fell exactly across the point we had difficulty locating on successive evenings. She used it to align the observations, and the measurements grew more consistent. There was nothing magical in this improvement. The scratch simply remained where the other marks had shifted. I kept the jar and began calling it the honest one. Anna found this description insufferable, though she continued to bring it whenever we discussed that series. Once I caught myself polishing around the scratch so carefully that the glass remained spotless except for the one flaw that had become useful. It was as much a portrait of my temper as any likeness Leonie ever made.

### E08-002 | The Photograph of a Hand | 1893-09

Anna took a photograph of her father's injured hand resting beside a newly finished glass joint. She intended it as a study of texture and asked whether I wished to examine the plate. The scar stood out pale against the dark wood; the delicate joint seemed almost impossibly thin. I suggested a second exposure with the hand withdrawn, for comparison. She made it and placed the two prints before me without comment. The joint appeared smaller in the first because the hand supplied a measure; in the second it looked like an object belonging to no scale at all. Anna said she would title the first 'The Maker.' Her father disliked the title and requested that she call it 'Thursday.' We settled on the date in the catalogue and left the family argument unresolved. I bought a copy and kept it beside Kappel's list of repaired instruments.

### E05-010 | The Sound Behind the Wall | 1893-11

A rhythmic tapping began behind the west chamber wall, usually after midnight. I assumed some animal had made a nest. Anna proposed that the newly fitted drain was transmitting movement from the outer gutter. Lenz tapped the pipe and reproduced the sound almost exactly. I was disappointed not to discover an intruder but satisfied to discover a mechanism. The tapping had disturbed the Ballerina's rest, and she had begun refusing music on the following mornings. We wrapped the pipe and shifted her vessel by a hand's breadth. The rhythm ceased and the dancing returned, though I recorded no claim of causation beyond the sequence. Anna asked whether the old professor still believed every peculiar noise was a plot. I said only the interesting ones.

### E24-010 | An Honest Catalogue of Imperfections | 1893-12

I wished to prepare a catalogue of the little court suitable for a visitor who had offered to purchase drawings. The Queen's profile deserved first place, followed by the Ballerina and the King. Anna placed her own list beside mine. Her headings were not beauty, proportion, and dignity but movement, tolerance of cold, appetite, and response to unfamiliar objects. The Devil, whom I had assigned an uncertain rank, led in two practical categories. I accused Anna of preparing an inventory of kitchen servants. She accused me of preparing a catalogue of porcelain. We retained both. A visiting collector favored my list and pronounced himself delighted. I found myself examining Anna's afterward. It had the intolerable merit of explaining why several apparently ungainly subjects survived difficulties that defeated their more finished companions.

### E08-003 | The Case of the Undead Sentence | 1894-01

Voss sent a note about an English editor who wanted the Queen's history reduced to three columns. Mathilde set a sample page. To fit the space she had removed the sentences in which I confessed uncertainty about the Queen's response to a familiar sound. Voss wrote that this made the piece more exciting and less true. I quite agreed, though I resented discovering that we had arrived together at so dreary a position. We restored the missing sentences and cut a more handsome passage concerning the insufficiency of modern universities. Its sacrifice hurt more than I should have expected. The editor declined the shortened article. Mathilde used the rejected setting to wrap a parcel of ordinary stationery, a fate that would have distressed a lesser author. I rescued one page before it could be folded around the ink bottles.

### E12-011 | The Room That Could Be Vacant | 1894-03

Clara missed our agreed Thursday visit, and Mathilde came to deliver pages that required signature. She found the second cup set out beside the kettle. I told her I had expected a clerk from town. She accepted this falsehood with the courtesy due a man behaving foolishly in his own house. We discussed the layout of a pamphlet, and she asked where the names of absent contributors were to appear. I said names belonged to the work actually done. She suggested that Clara's maintenance of the records had made the work possible, though few of her acts were included among the experiments. I added Clara's name to the acknowledgments and then objected to the word 'assistance' as insufficiently precise. We argued over a better phrase until the kettle boiled dry. I had preferred a vacant room to an obligation for many years. I kept the proof at my desk until Clara could examine the altered wording for herself.

### E21-012 | The Sphinx Page Used for Wrapping | 1894-05

Mathilde found an old issue of Sphinx and asked whether the remaining pages were worth preserving. One contained an argument about anomalous perception which I still wanted; the others included speculation so lavish that even I found it tiresome. I rescued the useful page and permitted the remainder to wrap a shipment of ordinary paper. When the parcel returned, the torn title had been stained purple with ink. I cut it away and kept the argument in my cabinet, where it joined much less glamorous material on sense and attention. It is tempting to declare that I thereby escaped occult fascination and returned to proper science. Nothing of the sort. I continued to read the strange literature. I merely grew less willing to pay for every claim merely because it came disguised as forbidden knowledge.

### E05-011 | The Farewell That Was Not One | 1894-07

Clara informed me that she intended to reduce her hours and transfer much of the business to an assistant. I immediately asked whether the tower could still obtain its customary supplies. She looked at me until I understood what I had made of the announcement. She was tired, she said, and wished to spend some mornings reading without a queue at her counter. I congratulated her poorly. On my next visit I brought a chair cushion, having noticed that the one beside the window had gone flat. We drank tea and discussed the weather. When I left, she asked that I continue calling even on days when I required nothing from the shop. I answered that I could not imagine why I should do otherwise. I began doing so the following Thursday.

### E24-011 | The Dreams I Could Not Manufacture | 1894-07

The glass door had appeared in my sleep often enough that I began to resent its independence. I arranged the same lamp, locked the same iron latch, and placed a scrap of frosted glass upon the bedside table. Nothing followed except an uncomfortable night and the smell of wet linen after the morning rain. I repeated the arrangement for a week. On the sixth morning I wrote down a fragment of a dream involving a railway ticket and an unknown child, neither of which suited my experiment. Voss asked whether I had considered allowing the dream to occur without permission. I accused her of proposing a research method fit for poets. I abandoned the apparatus. Months afterward the glass door returned in sleep, but I could not remember whether it opened. I suspect my irritation improved the memory less than I wished.

### E15-013 | The Name on the Seating Card | 1894-10

Voss persuaded me to attend a dinner at which an optical society intended to honor several instrument makers. Julian sat three places away. The organizer introduced him as a dealer and overlooked the restorative work for which he had been invited. I whispered that the evening would have benefited from a competent herald. Julian heard me, smiled, and then stood to correct the record on behalf of an elderly machinist at the far end of the table. He did not correct his own description. I found this excessive modesty irritating. When the speeches ended, I rose to propose a toast to the makers whose hands allowed theorists to remain immaculate. The wording pleased me enough that I very nearly spoiled it with a barb about committee chairs. Voss pressed a finger against her glass and I stopped. Julian thanked me later, saying the machinist had been touched. I replied that the speech had been intended to correct an error in the programme. He said this was precisely why it had mattered. I kept the seating card, though I pretended the paper was useful for notes.

### E08-004 | The Surgeon in a Yellow Book | 1894-12

A London volume containing Machen's *The Great God Pan* reached me through Mathilde's book trade. Here was another physician opening an alleged door between matter and spirit, with a patient made to bear the cost of his revelation. The tale was artful in its withholding of evidence and extravagant in its confidence about consequences. Voss asked why I had spent an entire evening with a book I called preposterous. I said I was studying the rhetoric by which a sensational author conceals the gaps in his argument. She pointed toward three of my own manuscripts lying unfinished on the table. I closed the book rather harder than necessary. Later I reopened it to the passage in which Raymond speaks of knowledge acquired through one irreversible act. I wrote in the margin that success in seeing was not success in understanding. I left the volume visible when Mathilde next called, which she interpreted quite correctly as an invitation to quarrel.

### E08-005 | The Unclaimed Seat | 1895-01

Clara had begun opening her shop late on Thursdays. I went at the former hour and found the front door locked, an event which my dignity ought to have endured without comment. Mathilde arrived carrying proofs and asked why I stood outside as though waiting for an execution. We went round to Clara's garden instead. She was reading in a chair and appeared neither ill nor repentant. I insisted that she should have changed the sign. She said she had changed it the previous month. I had merely stopped looking. We drank coffee at the garden table; Mathilde corrected a page; Clara returned to her book whenever the conversation grew tedious. The Thursday visits thereafter shifted to the garden if the weather permitted. I continued to call this an arrangement for reviewing proofs, although we reviewed almost none during the following summer.

### E05-012 | The Inventory of Failures | 1895-02

Anna proposed photographing every damaged joint before another order could repeat the mistake. Voss demanded a register of discarded hypotheses as well. Thus three afternoons vanished into the compilation of a formidable inventory of my misfortunes: warped vessels, false revivals, the Archbishop's misleading gestures, inconsistent growth, and one enormous container that would not pass through a door. Anna weighed the finished book against the catalogue of my published successes and found the former heavier. She expected me to be offended. I informed her that failures are naturally more numerous because most of nature is arranged to resist the improvement of philosophers. A month later the book saved us from repeating an optical error. I granted the catalogue a permanent place, although not on the shelf reserved for the achievements of which I was proudest.

### E12-012 | A Glass of Water before the Lecture | 1895-04

Reuter asked me to address a small gathering of physicians on the distinction between muscular response and sustained life. I prepared a splendid opening calculated to make their ordinary proceedings appear scarcely worth the hire of the room. Clara attended, though she had reduced her work at the dispensary. Before I spoke she handed me a glass of water and said that half my audience had been awake through the night. I began with an actual observation instead. The argument survived without my opening insult, and several physicians remained afterward to discuss the method. Reuter called the evening a success. I said it lacked its proper dramatic introduction. At home I wrote the abandoned opening into my private notes, where it remains pleasing, useless, and rather cruel to men who had given up their sleep to listen. I still enjoy the sentence. I simply did not deliver it.

### E08-006 | The Locked Cabinet of Reactions | 1895-06

Reuter asked whether the little people might become exhausted by repeated observation. I answered that I had already limited the trials. Anna brought out my schedule and showed that a day of 'rest' often contained several unscheduled visitors demanding a glimpse. I informed her that a visit was not necessarily an experiment. Reuter asked whether the subjects knew the distinction. We closed the west cabinet to casual display for one month, leaving its ordinary ventilation unchanged and entering each approach in a separate book. The visitors were displeased. The Queen grew no more demonstrably content, though her periods of retreat altered. I refused to call this proof of distress. I also refused to reopen the door when a gentleman from the town offered a considerable sum to look inside. Some boundaries are easier to defend when their breach has acquired a price.

### E18-007 | The Newspaper from London | 1895-06

News of Oscar Wilde's conviction reached us through the foreign papers. Mathilde laid a report upon her table and said that a man's entire life could be reduced to the word assigned him by a court. Julian had heard of the case before me. He seemed chiefly offended by the delight with which observers converted private correspondence into public entertainment. I examined the English statute and the reports as far as they were available, and found the legal machinery both efficient and indecently proud of itself. There were choices in the celebrated man's conduct which I did not admire. That did not make the state's right to arrange his humiliations self-evident. Julian asked whether I felt safe in Bavaria. I answered that safety was not an intellectual virtue. He said he had not asked whether it was virtuous. I changed the subject to a cracked prism. The discussion ended there, but I kept the newspaper folded among my scientific correspondence rather than with theatrical gossip. It was a reminder that observers do not require accuracy when they have found a spectacle that pleases them.

### E24-012 | A Visitor Who Wanted the Recipe | 1895-06

Otto Wernig arrived carrying an old treatise wrapped in dry leather. He expected me to acknowledge a debt to its author. I was inclined to deny any resemblance between ancient recipes and the actual discipline of my work. Yet the marginal drawing he showed me resembled one of my discarded chamber designs closely enough to spoil the afternoon. We spent hours comparing vocabulary, separating what an alchemist might have meant by seed from what I had observed in matter. He asked where the resemblance ended. I told him that a bibliography does not entitle a bookseller to a formula. He accepted the distinction and requested a cigar. I refused to surrender my last good one, which proved to be a greater offense. He left with a catalogue of works to find. I retained the treatise and entered his name beside a debt, not a revelation.

### E21-013 | The Creature with the Unfashionable Nose | 1895-08

Adler showed me the profile of a developing figure whose nose would not have been permitted upon any classical bust. I had already drawn three proposed improvements. Clara asked whether its behavior was defective. It was not. The creature recognized the approach of a familiar hand, avoided a disagreeable vibration, and returned to its ordinary place when left alone. I had no defensible reason to improve its nose except that I disliked the outline. So I forbade the alteration and had the drawings put away. Adler looked at me with an expression dangerously near satisfaction. I reminded him that ugliness remained ugly regardless of one's moral principles. He agreed and went back to his work. I spent the evening imagining how little effort might have been required to make the face agreeable.

### E05-013 | A New Instrument, an Old Bias | 1895-10

Faber's new timing instrument could divide an interval with exquisite delicacy. I used it upon the King's movements and announced improved regularity. Voss examined the original records and found I had shifted the instant at which each interval began. The correction disposed of my discovery. Faber wrote that even a perfect mechanism could not cure an observer of imprecision. I placed his note beside the dial, where guests might admire the wit without knowing its victim. Privately I recalculated three old experiments and discovered that one result, dismissed years before, had been more substantial than I believed. I did not announce a triumph. I marked the page for repetition. Instruments sometimes correct a man's conceit and sometimes vindicate his suspicion; only the first outcome is generally told with pleasure by his friends.

### E24-013 | A Refusal That Cost Something | 1895-12

Julian had arranged an excursion in winter, a week away from instruments and the incessant improvisations by which I kept my household running. I had agreed months before. At the station café I announced that the Queen required observation during an uncertain transition. Julian reminded me that I had already appointed Anna to observe her. Outside, steam drifted beneath the lamps and wet passengers shook rain from their coats. He peeled an orange with the care he gave to repairing a delicate hinge. He asked whether I had simply decided not to come. I answered with a discussion of risk that would have persuaded any reasonable colleague. He finished the orange, put on a dry glove, and said he would make the journey alone. I watched his train depart. The experiment proceeded without my presence for part of the following day. It remained inconclusive.

### E08-007 | The Hand Beneath the Rays | 1896-02

Reports of Röntgen's new rays had circulated for weeks when Anna persuaded us to attend a demonstration of their photographic effects. A hand appeared upon the plate with its bones pale against the dark, stripped of the familiar flesh that makes a gesture recognizable. Anna studied the image longer than I did. I asked what use could be made of the method for examining sealed vessels without disturbing their contents. Voss asked what evidence I imagined a shadow would supply about life. We returned with no apparatus, only an illustrated notice and a disagreeably large collection of questions. I laid Anna's photograph of her father's hand beside the new printed plate. One celebrated surface; the other disclosed a structure concealed beneath it. Neither told me why Lenz continued to labor with a finger that pained him. I did not mention this last deficiency to the demonstrator.

### E18-008 | His Work on the Wall | 1896-02
